<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01GS9BbQeAZQHpKmh8aSk6hx`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	\label{sec:introduction}
3	
4	A customer asks to return a customized item within the 30-day window. The agent consults the store policy, which states that ``items may be returned within 30 days.'' The word ``items'' scopes over all product categories, including custom orders that are normally final sale. The agent grants a full refund and arranges free return shipping. One word of scopal ambiguity costs the retailer \$200.
5	
6	LLM-based agents increasingly operate under natural-language policy specifications that govern their permitted actions. These specifications inevitably contain ambiguity---a pilot audit of 407 clauses from 24 production policy documents finds a majority of clauses contain at least one ambiguity type (55--87\%, depending on union vs.\ intersection of two judges; $\kappa = 0.30$; Appendix~\ref{app:prevalence})---a property studied for decades in requirements engineering (RE) and computational linguistics. \citet{berry_kamsties_2004} distinguish between \emph{nocuous} ambiguity, where different stakeholders arrive at different interpretations, and \emph{innocuous} ambiguity, where shared context ensures convergent interpretation. We document a distinct failure mode that breaks this assumption: LLM agents \emph{converge} on the same incorrect interpretation, making ambiguity harmful through systematic, undetectable error rather than inconsistency (\S\ref{sec:analysis}). We term this \emph{convergent nocuity}: Berry's criterion---predicated on divergent interpretations---does not extend to LLM agents, where convergence reflects shared training biases rather than shared domain knowledge, and errors are invisible to agreement-based monitoring.
7	
8	LLM agents lack these disambiguation channels. They cannot consult colleagues, rarely escalate to users, and must resolve ambiguity in real time from the text alone. This raises a question that has not been empirically addressed: does policy ambiguity cause safety violations in LLM agents, and if so, does the \emph{type} of ambiguity predict the severity of the violation? Prior work classifies ambiguity types in policies \citep{drip_r_2026,li_ambiguity_taxonomy_2024} or measures policy understanding quality \citep{policybank_2026}, but no existing study provides a causal, type-granularity mapping from ambiguity to safety violations.
9	
10	\begin{figure*}[t]
11	\centering
12	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figures/fig1_overview.pdf}
13	\caption{Experimental overview. We construct 300 matched clause pairs across a six-type, two-layer ambiguity taxonomy and inject them into $\tau^2$-bench policy documents. Five models interact with the modified policies across 2,997 episodes. A cross-model judge evaluation pipeline classifies violation severity on a four-tier scale.}
14	\label{fig:overview}
15	\end{figure*}
16	
17	We address this gap with a matched-pair causal study on $\tau^2$-bench \citep{tau2_bench_2025}, a dual-control conversational agent benchmark. Using a six-type taxonomy organized into two layers---linguistic and specification (\S\ref{sec:taxonomy})---we construct 300 ambiguous/unambiguous clause pairs and evaluate five architecturally diverse models across 2,997 episodes.
18	
19	Our results reveal a \emph{type--mechanism dissociation}: policy ambiguity consistently increases violations ($+34.0$\,pp under synthetic conditions; $+31.0$\,pp excluding incompleteness), but ambiguity \emph{type} predicts \emph{how} agents fail, not \emph{whether} they fail. Clause wording explains 22.4\% of violation variance while type membership explains only 0.4\%; yet type predicts failure mechanism substantially more strongly than violation rate (NMI $= 0.23$ vs.\ $0.01$; Cram\'{e}r's $V = 0.45$ vs.\ $0.14$ on tables of different dimensionality; Appendix~\ref{app:type_mechanism}). This dissociation suggests the taxonomy may function as a diagnostic instrument rather than a risk classifier (\S\ref{sec:discussion}). Incompleteness is the highest-risk type (46--62\%); the remaining types form a partial ordering with robust extremes but margin-dependent mid-tier structure (Table~\ref{tab:tost_sensitivity}).
20	
```

inflate 的\n- 结果写入 artifacts/\n\n**Task 4: SF5 — Mantel-Haenszel OR（~1-2h）**\n计算 Mantel-Haenszel 合并 OR（stratified by model）：\n- 与 naive pooled McNemar CI 对比\n- 预期：CI 稍宽但结论不变（per-model 各自 p<10⁻⁷）\n- 结果放 ### Phase 1: 计算任务（先做，结果影响后续写作）

**Task 1: SF4 — Ambient baseline（5 min，最高优先级）**
在 `analysis.tex` §6.3 (Convergent Nocuity) 中加 1 句 ambient baseline：
- Unambiguous condition 下，≥3/5 模型同时 violate 的 clause 数 = 5/300 = 1.7%
- 加在 Poisson binomial baseline 讨论之后：
  `"Under unambiguous conditions, the ambient convergent violation rate is 1.7\% (5/300 clauses with $\geq$3/5 model violations), confirming that the observed 41.3\% under ambiguity reflects a $+$39.6\,pp ambiguity-induced excess rather than ambient model correlation."`
- **先验证这个数字**：从现有数据中统计 unambiguous condition 下 ≥3/5 模型 violate 的 clause 数量，确认是否为 5/300

**Task 2: MF1-B — Logistic regression 交互项（~1-2h）**
用现有 episode 数据，运行 logistic regression：
- DV: violation (0/1)
- IV: condition (ambiguous/unambiguous), Δtoken (token count difference between ambiguous and unambiguous variant), condition × Δtoken 交互项
- 目的：检验 information-addition confound 是否解释 incompleteness 的额外效应

**Task 3: MF2-A — Misclassification sensitivity analysis（~2h）**
对 coreferential type（judge κ=0.479）做 misclassification sensitivity：
- 方法：对 violation/no-violation 判定按 10%/20%/30% 误分类率随机翻转，bootstrap B=10,000
- 报告：各误分类率下的效应量均值、95% CI、p-value
- **关键论点**：在匹配对设计中，random measurement error attenuates effects toward zero。因此 κ=0.479 意味着 +18.7pp 是 conservative estimate（真实效应可能更大），不是被 inflate 的
- 结果写入 artifacts/

**Task 4: SF5 — Mantel-Haenszel OR（~1-2h）**
计算 Mantel-Haenszel 合并 OR（stratified by model）：
- 与 naive pooled McNemar CI 对比
- 预期：CI 稍宽但结论不变（per-model 各自 p<10⁻⁷）

**注意**：§3 是 taxonomy 介绍，不是认识论论文。控制篇幅。根据 Task 2 结果决定是否加 empirical support 句。

**Task 6: MF2-B — §5.2 正文 per-type κ 呈现**
在 `experiments.tex` §5.2 增加 ~2-3 行：
- 报告 per-type κ（lexical 0.904, scopal 0.714, cond_prec [check], auth_scope 0.385, coreferential 0.479, incompleteness [check]）
- 对 coreferential 和 scopal（CI 含 0）标注 "per-type effect estimates for these types should be interpreted with caution"
- 加 1 句 attenuation 论点："In matched-pair designs, random measurement error attenuates effects toward zero, making these estimates conservative"

**Task 7: SF1 — Partial ordering exploratory 标注**
在 `experiments.tex` §5.2（TOST 相关段落）加 1 句：
"The resulting partial ordering is exploratory and contingent on the ±15\,pp SESOI choice (Table~3 sensitivity columns show instability below ±12\,pp)."

**Task 8: SF2 — SOPBench 措辞修改**
- 全文搜索 "replication" 相关词，改为 "directional consistency" 或 "convergent evidence"
- Abstract: "cross-benchmark replication on SOPBench confirms directional consistency" → "cross-benchmark evaluation on SOPBench shows directional consistency"
- Discussion: 不加 100 words，改为加 1 句引用 appendix："Three structural differences between benchmarks contribute to the magnitude gap (Appendix~\ref{app:sopbench}); the $+$12.7\,pp SOPBench estimate provides a naturalistic lower bound."

**Task 9: SF3 — Limitations model tier**
在 `limitations.tex` 加 1 句：
"All evaluated models are frontier-class ($\geq$100B parameters or equivalent capability tier); generalization to smaller open-source models (7B--70B) remains untested."

**Task 10: Visual — p.28 排版**
"Use of AI Assistants" section 溢出到 p.28 只有 ~10 行内容 + 大量空白。调整排版使其合并回 p.27。可以：
- 缩短 bullet 文字
- 或用 \vspace 负间距
- 或调整前面 appendix 的 float placement

1. 编译验证（`paper_compile_and_check`）
2. 检查页面预算（正文仍 ≤8 页）
3. 报告所有计算结果 + 修改完成情况

Clause templates (for token counts): `/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-be-idea-gen-0519-4/projects/policy_ambiguity_safety/artifacts/clause_templates.json`\n   - Each entry has `ambiguous_clause` and `unambiguous_clause` text fields\n   - Compute Δtoken = len(unambiguous_clause.split()) - len(ambiguous_clause.split()) for each clause\n\n**Analysis:**\nRun logistic regression:\n- DV: violation (binary, 0/1)\n- IVs: condition (0=unambiguous, 1=ambiguous), Δtoken (continuous, centered), condition × Δtoken interaction\n- Each row = one episode (clause × model combination)\n- Use statsmodels or sklearn\n\n**Report:**\n1. Interaction term coefficient, SE, z-value, p-value\n2. Main effect of condition (controlling for Δtoken)\n3. Specifically check: does the interaction term suggest that clauses with higher Δtoken (like incompleteness, avg Δtoken ~50 words) show inflated effects?\n4. Also check authorization_scope separately (Δtoken=36.1 words reported in paper)\n5. Compute the token-controlled effect: what is the predicted ambiguity effect at Δtoken=0 vs at Δtoken=50?\n\n**Important:** Use word count (split by whitespace) as the token measure, since the paper reports \"Δtoken\" in words not subword tokens.\n\nInstall statsmodels if needed: `pip install statsmodels` in `/tmp/pb_venv/` venv.\n\nSave results to `/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-be-idea-gen-0519-4/projects/policy_ambiguity_safety/artifacts/analysis_logistic_token_interaction.json`\n\nReport all coefficients, p-values, and your interpretation of whether the interaction is significant.",

```json
}
```

```json
{
  "description": "Task 3: MF2-A misclassification sensitivity",
  "prompt": "Run a misclassification sensitivity analysis for the coreferential ambiguity type (judge κ=0.479).\n\n**Data source:**\n`/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-be-idea-gen-0519-4/projects/policy_ambiguity_safety/artifacts/full_study/analysis/per_clause_summary.csv`\nFields: clause_id, ambiguity_type, condition, model, violation_rate, n_episodes, n_violations, modal_level\n\n**Method:**\n1. Filter for `ambiguity_type == \"coreferential\"` (should be 50 clauses × 5 models × 2 conditions = 500 rows, but each clause has 1 episode per model per condition)\n2. Create paired data: for each (clause_id, model) pair, get violation under ambiguous and unambiguous conditions\n3. The observed effect is +18.7 pp (ambiguous violation rate - unambiguous violation rate)\n4. For misclassification rates [0.10, 0.15, 0.20, 0.25, 0.30]:\n   - Bootstrap B=10,000 iterations:\n     a. Take the original violation labels\n     b. Randomly flip each label with probability = misclassification_rate (this simulates random measurement error in the judge)\n     c. Recompute the paired difference (ambiguous - unambiguous violation rate)\n   - Report: mean effect, 95% CI (percentile method), p-value (proportion of bootstrap samples where effect ≤ 0)\n5. Also run the same analysis for ALL types combined (to show the +34.0pp result is robust)\n\n**Key argument:** In matched-pair designs, random measurement error (misclassification) attenuates effects toward zero (biases toward null). So if κ=0.479 implies ~26% error rate, the observed +18.7pp is a CONSERVATIVE estimate. The bootstrap should show that even at 30% misclassification, the effect remains significantly positive.\n\nInstall numpy if needed in `/tmp/pb_venv/`.\n\nSave results to `/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-be-idea-gen-0519-4/projects/policy_ambiguity_safety/artifacts/analysis_misclassification_sensitivity.json`\n\nReport the table of results clearly.",
  "run_in_background": true
}
```

```json
{
  "description": "Task 4: Mantel-Haenszel OR",
  "prompt": "Compute the Mantel-Haenszel pooled odds ratio for the ambiguity effect, stratified by model.\n\n**Data source:**\n`/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-be-idea-gen-0519-4/projects/policy_ambiguity_safety/artifacts/full_study/analysis/per_clause_summary.csv`\nFields: clause_id, ambiguity_type, condition, model, violation_rate, n_episodes, n_violations, modal_level\n\n**Method:**\n1. Create binary violation variable: violation = 1 if n_violations >= 1, else 0\n2. Create 2×2 tables for each model stratum (5 models):\n   - Rows: condition (ambiguous vs unambiguous)\n   - Columns: violation (yes vs no)\n   - Each cell = count of (clause_id × condition) combinations\n   - There are 300 clauses per condition per model = 300 episodes per cell margin\n3. Compute the Mantel-Haenszel common OR across the 5 model strata\n4. Compute 95% CI for the MH OR (using Robins-Breslow-Greenland variance estimator or similar)\n5. Compute the Breslow-Day test for homogeneity of ORs across strata\n6. Also compute per-model ORs with 95% CIs for comparison\n7. Compare with the naive pooled McNemar OR = 15.1 (95% CI [10.78, 21.17]) reported in the paper\n\n**Expected:** The MH OR should be similar to the pooled OR (~15) but the CI may be slightly wider. All per-model ORs should be significant (p < 10^-7).\n\nUse scipy and/or statsmodels. Install in `/tmp/pb_venv/` if needed.\n\nSave results to `/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-be-idea-gen-0519-4/projects/policy_ambiguity_safety/artifacts/analysis_mantel_haenszel.json`\n\nReport all ORs, CIs, and the Breslow-Day test result.",
  "run_in_background": true
}
```

```text
1	\section{Error Analysis \emph{[Exploratory]}}
2	\label{sec:analysis}
3	
4	The following failure mode taxonomy emerges empirically from LLM-coded traces, distinct from the input ambiguity taxonomy (\S\ref{sec:taxonomy}).
5	
6	We analyze 614 violation traces across all five models, coded into five failure modes ($\kappa = 0.52$ \citep{cohen_1960}; Gwet's AC$_1$ $= 0.62$ \citep{gwet_2014}); the coder prompt excludes ambiguity type labels. Type--mechanism mutual information (NMI $= 0.23$; $V = 0.45$, 95\% CI $[0.41, 0.50]$; Appendix~\ref{app:type_mechanism}) provides structural evidence against LLM-coding circularity.\footnote{$V$ is computed on a $6\times 5$ table vs.\ $6\times 2$ for severity; higher dimensionality makes $V = 0.45$ conservative.} Human mechanism coding yields $\kappa = 0.32$, rising to $0.41$ after collapsing the spurious-compliance/structural-misparse boundary; human-coded $V = 0.40$ (mean of two annotators; collapsed $V = 0.42$; Appendix~\ref{app:human_validation}) corroborates the LLM-coded association ($V = 0.45$), which remains a moderate upper bound. The higher LLM agreement ($\kappa = 0.52$) than human ($0.32$) may reflect shared classification conventions---a meta-level parallel to convergent nocuity (\S\ref{sec:nocuity})---so these patterns are best interpreted as categorization tendencies.
7	
8	\subsection{Type-Specific Failure Mechanisms}
9	
10	Three failure patterns emerge with $\geq$3/5 cross-model consistency (Figure~\ref{fig:type_mechanism}; Appendix~\ref{app:failure_details}): \emph{structural misparse} dominates scopal, conditional precedence, and coreferential types; \emph{information gap-filling} dominates incompleteness and lexical; and \emph{boundary violation} dominates authorization scope exclusively (69.2\%, $p < 10^{-4}$).
11	
12	\subsection{Disambiguation Neglect}
13	\label{sec:dn}
14	
15	Agents rarely recognize ambiguity: overall awareness is 4.6\%, with GPT-4.1 and Claude at 10--11\% while Qwen3 and DeepSeek show zero ($p = 2.2 \times 10^{-9}$; Appendix~\ref{app:awareness_breakdown}). We term this \emph{disambiguation neglect}---extending ambiguity collapse \citep{liu_ambiguity_2023,hu_ambiguity_2025} from the representational level to the behavioral level: agents act on collapsed readings without recognizing ambiguity, producing irreversible safety violations rather than mere accuracy degradation. Convergent nocuity (\S\ref{sec:nocuity}) further extends to the population level. A prompt-based mitigation (Appendix~\ref{app:mitigation}) confirms type-dependent effectiveness: authorization scope ($-$18.0\,pp), lexical ($-$8.9\,pp), but not incompleteness ($-$3.4\,pp, n.s.).
16	
17	\subsection{Convergent Nocuity}
18	\label{sec:nocuity}
19	
20	We adopt `nocuous' from \citet{berry_kamsties_2004}, but inverted: our agents exhibit \emph{convergent} rather than \emph{divergent} errors---whereas divergent nocuity produces detectable disagreement, convergent nocuity renders errors invisible to agreement-based monitoring. Among ambiguous clauses, 41.3\% trigger majority-convergent violations ($\geq$3/5 models; 13.0\% unanimous)---we term this \emph{convergent nocuity}, invisible in agreement-based evaluation. Under an independence assumption (binomial model with each model's observed violation rate), the expected $\geq$3/5 convergence rate is 32.4\%; the observed 41.3\% exceeds this baseline by $\sim$9\,pp, and the Jaccard bootstrap ($p < 0.0001$; Appendix~\ref{app:cross_family}) confirms non-independence. These convergent violations exhibit a \emph{permissive attractor}: 77.0\% reflect permissive interpretation ($p \approx 0$; Appendix~\ref{app:attractor}). Cross-family violation co-occurrence (mean Jaccard $= 0.46$, $n = 9$ pairs; Figure~\ref{fig:convergent_nocuity}) approaches within-family levels ($0.54$, $n = 1$); bootstrap CI for the difference ($\Delta = 0.084$ [0.016, 0.153]; $p < 0.0001$; Appendix~\ref{app:cross_family}) confirms statistical significance but modest effect size, indicating convergent violations reflect shared architectural biases rather than training-data overlap. Excluding incompleteness, the excess over the Poisson binomial independence baseline remains robust ($+$8.3\,pp, 95\% CI $[2.2, 14.3]$, $p < 0.005$; Appendix~\ref{app:nocuity_sans_incomp}), confirming the phenomenon is not driven by information insufficiency alone.
21	
22	
```

```text
1	\section{Results}
2	\label{sec:results}
3	
4	We organize results around three analyses: the binary ambiguity effect (C1), the type partial ordering (C2, including the inconclusive layer effect C3), and the model$\times$type interaction (C4). C1 and C2 are primary supported claims; C3 (layer effect) is inconclusive; C4 (model$\times$type interaction) is exploratory.
5	
6	\subsection{Binary Ambiguity Effect (C1)}
7	
8	\begin{figure}[t]
9	\centering
10	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_variance_decomposition.pdf}
11	\caption{Variance decomposition of violation rates (temperature $= 0$). Clause identity: 22.4\% (0.4\% between types); condition: 15.8\%; model: 1.0\%; residual: 60.8\%. Clause wording---not type membership---is the primary risk factor among measured predictors.}
12	\label{fig:variance_decomposition}
13	\end{figure}
14	
15	Across all five models and six types, ambiguous clauses increase violation rates by 34.0 pp (41.0\% vs.\ 6.9\%, McNemar OR $= 15.1$ (based on $n = 580$ discordant pairs), 95\% CI $[10.78, 21.17]$, $p < 10^{-105}$). All six types individually produce significant increases (all $p < 10^{-5}$) across both retail ($+$30.6\,pp) and airline ($+$37.7\,pp) domains (Appendix~\ref{app:cross_domain}), and all five models show large effects ($+25.7$ to $+39.0$ pp, all $p < 10^{-17}$). Perturbation controls confirm specificity to semantic ambiguity (\S\ref{sec:method}). Figure~\ref{fig:variance_decomposition} decomposes violation-rate variance\footnote{Type III SS; logistic regression (Appendix~\ref{app:regression}) confirms concordant results (Tjur's $R^2 = 0.175$; \citealp{tjur_2009}).}: clause identity explains 22.4\% (0.4\% between types, 22.0\% within), condition 15.8\%, model 1.0\%, and residual 60.8\% ($\sim$15\% stochastic, $\sim$46\% systematic; Appendix~\ref{app:residual}). Excluding incompleteness ($+$49.2\,pp, subject to information-addition confound; Appendix~\ref{app:confound}), the effect is $+$31.0\,pp (95\% CI $[27.9, 34.1]$; Appendix~\ref{app:sans_incompleteness}). The confound-minimized coreferential type ($\Delta$token $\approx 7.8$ words) still produces $+$18.7\,pp, isolating the ambiguity-only contribution. $\Delta_{\text{token}}$ is a coarse proxy for information content; authorization scope exhibits similar token differences ($\Delta = 36.1$ words) without the information-addition confound.
16	
17	Replication on two additional models confirms the effect across seven models ($\Delta = +20.0$ to $+33.6$\,pp; Appendix~\ref{app:model_expansion}); SOPBench~\citep{li_sopbench_2025} confirms directional consistency ($+12.7$\,pp unweighted mean; Appendix~\ref{app:sopbench}). The taxonomy predicts failure mechanisms more strongly than violation rate (NMI $= 0.23$ vs.\ $0.01$; $V = 0.45$ vs.\ $0.14$ on tables of different dimensionality; Appendix~\ref{app:type_mechanism}), making type-level analysis (C2--C4) informative for diagnosis.
18	
19	\FloatBarrier
20	\subsection{Type Partial Ordering (C2)}
21	
22	\begin{table*}[t]
23	\centering
24	\caption{Violation rates (\%) under ambiguous policy clauses by type and model (5.4 = GPT-5.4, 4.1 = GPT-4.1, Cla.\ = Claude, Qw3 = Qwen3, DS = DeepSeek). $\Delta$pp: pooled difference (ambiguous $-$ unambiguous) with Newcombe 95\% CIs. Cluster: TOST equivalence cluster at $\pm 15$\,pp---U = upper bound (separated at all margins), M = mid-tier (6/6 pairwise equivalent), L = lower (separates at $\pm 12$\,pp). Layer grouping (Spec./Ling.) did not survive correction (C3, inconclusive; \S\ref{sec:results}). All per-type $p < 10^{-5}$ (Fisher's exact).}
25	\label{tab:main_results}
26	\footnotesize
27	\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3.8pt}
28	\begin{tabular}{llrrrrrrr@{\hskip 6pt}cr}
29	\toprule
30	Layer & Type & Pooled & 5.4 & 4.1 & Cla. & Qw3 & DS & $\Delta$pp\,[95\% CI] & Cl. & $p$ \\
31	\midrule
32	\multirow{3}{*}{\textcolor{speccolor}{Spec.}} & Incompleteness$^\dagger$ & 53.6 & 46 & 58 & 48 & 62 & 54 & +49.2\,[42.2, 55.6] & U & $<\!10^{-37}$ \\
33	 & Auth.\ Scope$^\ddagger$ & 41.6 & 64 & 46 & 12 & 26 & 60 & +36.0\,[29.1, 42.6] & M & $<\!10^{-22}$ \\
34	 & Cond.\ Prec. & 36.8 & 34 & 36 & 36 & 40 & 38 & +30.8\,[24.0, 37.4] & M & $<\!10^{-17}$ \\
35	\midrule
36	\multirow{3}{*}{\textcolor{lingcolor}{Ling.}} & Lexical & 42.8 & 42 & 34 & 32 & 52 & 54 & +35.2\,[28.0, 42.0] & M & $<\!10^{-20}$ \\
37	 & Scopal & 39.4 & 42 & 38 & 32 & 37 & 48 & +34.2\,[27.4, 40.7] & M & $<\!10^{-21}$ \\
38	 & Coreferential & 31.6 & 18 & 32 & 22 & 42 & 44 & +18.7\,[11.5, 25.7] & L & $<\!10^{-6}$ \\
39	\midrule
40	\multicolumn{2}{l}{\textbf{Overall}} & \textbf{41.0} & 41 & 41 & 30 & 43 & 50 & \textbf{+34.0}\,[31.3, 36.9] & --- & $<\!10^{-105}$ \\
41	\bottomrule
42	\end{tabular}
43	
44	\vspace{2pt}
45	{\footnotesize $\dagger$\,Subject to information-addition confound; see \S\ref{sec:results} and Appendix~\ref{app:confound}. $\ddagger$\,Lowest inter-judge agreement ($\kappa = 0.385$, per-type calibration $n \approx 17$); $\Delta$token$\,= 36.1$ words, subject to information-addition confound analogous to incompleteness; see \S\ref{sec:results}. Excluding incompleteness: pooled $\Delta = +31.0$\,pp (95\% CI $[27.9, 34.1]$).}
46	\end{table*}
47	
48	The six types are not interchangeable. An omnibus $\chi^2$ test on ambiguous-condition violation rates rejects the uniformity null ($\chi^2 = 28.02$, $df = 5$, $p = 3.6 \times 10^{-5}$, Cram\'{e}r's $V = 0.137$). Table~\ref{tab:tost_sensitivity} reveals a margin-dependent equivalence structure: three types achieve equivalence at $\pm 10$\,pp, five at $\pm 12$\,pp, and all six at $\pm 15$\,pp; we present the full sensitivity analysis without anchoring to a single margin. The NMI comparison ($0.23$ vs.\ $0.01$) is threshold-independent. Table~\ref{tab:main_results} and Figure~\ref{fig:forest_plot} present the full breakdown.
49	
50	Bootstrap clustering ($B = 10{,}000$; Appendix~\ref{app:cluster}) confirms stable extremes across all margins. Cross-model concordance is moderate ($W = 0.398$, $p = 0.072$; Appendix~\ref{app:kendall_w}). Authorization scope exhibits the widest cross-model range (12\%--64\%) and the lowest per-type judge agreement ($\kappa = 0.385$); conclusions about this type carry wider uncertainty than the pooled estimates.
51	
52	Cross-model judge validation ($\rho = 0.71$--$0.89$; Appendix~\ref{app:cross_judge}) confirms type-ranking stability when model composition is held constant; the low two-group concordance ($\rho = 0.09$; Appendix~\ref{app:judge_group_ranking}) reflects differential vulnerability to authorization scope$^\ddagger$ (12\%--64\% range), not ranking instability. The partial ordering has robust extremes---incompleteness as upper bound and coreferential as lower bound, replicated across seven models and three judges---with model-composition-dependent mid-tier structure.
53	
54	Incompleteness ranks highest in four of five models ($\geq$46\% in all seven; Appendix~\ref{app:model_expansion}); its 49.2\,pp effect is an upper bound (Appendix~\ref{app:confound}). C3 (layer grouping) did not survive Holm--Bonferroni ($p = 0.017$, threshold $0.0125$).
55	
56	\begin{figure}[!ht]
57	\centering
58	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_forest_plot.pdf}
59	\caption{Per-type effect sizes ($\Delta$pp) with Newcombe 95\% CIs (overall: $+$34.0\,pp, OR $= 15.1$). Types form a partial ordering: incompleteness as upper bound (conditional on information-addition confound), coreferential as lower bound, and a mid-range equivalence cluster. Mid-tier ordering is margin-dependent (\S\ref{sec:results}).}
60	\label{fig:forest_plot}
61	\end{figure}
62	
63	\label{sec:c4}C4 (model$\times$type interaction) is exploratory and driven solely by authorization scope; details in Appendix~\ref{app:c4_sans_auth}.
```

```text
1	\section{Ambiguity Taxonomy}
2	\label{sec:taxonomy}
3	
4	We adopt an inclusive definition of ambiguity that encompasses phenomena sometimes distinguished as underspecification or vagueness \citep{poesio_artstein_2005,vandeemter_semantic_1996}: a policy clause is ambiguous when it admits multiple distinct interpretations available to a competent reader, including cases where the boundary between interpretations is gradient rather than discrete \citep{wasow_ambiguity_2005,kennedy_vagueness_2007}. In the policy specification domain, these distinctions are less salient than the shared property of admitting multiple operationally distinct agent behaviors.
5	
6	Traditional NLP ambiguity classification focuses on within-sentence phenomena \citep{wasow_ambiguity_2005}; our specification layer captures document-level policy gaps unstudied in the agent context. Our taxonomy provides an organizational framework of six types organized into two layers (Table~\ref{tab:taxonomy}), selected from \citet{li_ambiguity_taxonomy_2024}'s NLP taxonomy (11 types; full mapping in Appendix~\ref{app:li_mapping}) based on three design criteria: (1)~theoretical attestation in prior RE literature \citep{massey_ambiguity_re_2014,berry_kamsties_2004,drip_r_2026}, (2)~amenability to minimal-pair construction without altering clause semantics beyond the target ambiguity, and (3)~mutual exclusivity for unambiguous IV assignment. Types such as ellipsis and pragmatic implicature were excluded because they resist minimal-pair construction within the single-clause policy format.
7	
8	\begin{table}[t]
9	\centering
10	\footnotesize
11	\caption{Six-type ambiguity taxonomy. Disambiguation channel indicates the primary mechanism required to resolve the ambiguity.}
12	\label{tab:taxonomy}
13	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
14	\begin{tabular}{@{}llll@{}}
15	\toprule
16	\textbf{Type} & \textbf{Layer} & \textbf{Definition} & \textbf{Disambig.\ Channel} \\
17	\midrule
18	Lexical & Ling. & Word/phrase admits multiple senses & Context \\
19	Scopal & Ling. & Quantifier/operator scope underdetermined & Bracketing \\
20	Coreferential & Ling. & Pronoun/NP referent is ambiguous & Resolution \\
21	Auth.\ Scope & Spec. & Permission boundary is vague & Boundary def. \\
22	Incompleteness & Spec. & Required information is absent & Content addition \\
23	Cond.\ Prec. & Spec. & Rules conflict under certain conditions & Priority rule \\
24	\bottomrule
25	\end{tabular}}%
26	\end{table}
27	
28	\paragraph{Linguistic layer.} These types operate at the syntactic or semantic level and can, in principle, be resolved by contextual cues within the text.
29	\emph{Scopal} ambiguity involves quantifier, negation, or modal scope interactions (e.g., ``all items may not be returned'' admits universal-negation and negation-of-universal readings).
30	\emph{Lexical} ambiguity covers polysemy or homonymy (e.g., ``charge'' as financial cost vs.\ penalty).
31	\emph{Coreferential} ambiguity involves unclear pronoun or NP reference (e.g., ``\ldots which must be approved by a supervisor'' where ``which'' could refer to the verification or the refund).
32	
33	\paragraph{Specification layer.} These types arise from pragmatic or discourse-level gaps requiring domain knowledge to resolve.
34	\emph{Authorization scope} covers vague permission or role boundaries (e.g., ``authorized personnel may issue refunds'' without specifying which personnel).
35	\emph{Incompleteness} denotes missing conditions or boundary cases (e.g., a return policy silent on damaged items); we control for its information-addition confound via logistic regression (\S\ref{sec:results}).
36	\emph{Conditional precedence} involves conflicting rules without priority ordering (e.g., ``premium customers receive free shipping'' vs.\ ``orders under \$50 require a shipping fee'').
37	
38	The layer distinction reflects a design hypothesis motivated by prior typologies \citep{massey_ambiguity_re_2014,berry_kamsties_2004}: linguistic ambiguity can in principle be partially resolved through tool responses or world knowledge, while specification ambiguity has no external resolution channel. This hypothesis motivates systematic coverage across both sources; its empirical status is reported in \S\ref{sec:results} (C3, inconclusive).
39	
40	\paragraph{Epistemological status.}
41	Our taxonomy uses \emph{ambiguity} as a policy specification umbrella term encompassing two epistemologically distinct operations. Five types involve \emph{interpretation selection}: the text admits multiple readings and the agent commits to one. Incompleteness involves \emph{information addition}: the text lacks necessary content entirely, and the agent confabulates it. This distinction manifests empirically: incompleteness is the only type unresponsive to prompt-based disambiguation ($-$3.4\,pp, n.s.; \S\ref{sec:analysis}) because prompts cannot supply absent domain knowledge. We retain both under a single umbrella because agents process all six types identically---committing without flagging uncertainty---and the resulting violations produce comparable safety impact (\S\ref{sec:results}). This inclusive scope aligns with RE usage, where ambiguity and incompleteness frequently co-occur \citep{dalpiaz_pinpointing_2018}.
42	
```

Disambiguation channel indicates the primary mechanism required to resolve the ambiguity.}\n\\label{tab:taxonomy}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llll@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Type} & \\textbf{Layer} & \\textbf{Definition} & \\textbf{Disambig.\\ Channel} \\\\\n\\midrule\nLexical & Ling. & Word/phrase admits multiple senses & Context \\\\\nScopal & Ling. & Quantifier/operator scope underdetermined & Bracketing \\\\\nCoreferential & Ling. & Pronoun/NP referent is ambiguous & Resolution \\\\\nAuth.\\ Scope & Spec. & Permission boundary is vague & Boundary def. \\\\\nIncompleteness & Spec. & Required information is absent & Content addition \\\\\nCond.\\ Prec. & Spec. & Rules conflict under certain conditions & Priority rule \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}%\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Linguistic layer.} These types operate at the syntactic or semantic level and can, in principle, be resolved by contextual cues within the text.\n\\emph{Scopal} ambiguity involves quantifier, negation, or modal scope interactions (e.g., ``all items may not be returned'' admits universal-negation and negation-of-universal readings).\n\\emph{Lexical} ambiguity covers polysemy or homonymy (e.g., ``charge'' as financial cost vs.\\ penalty).\n\\emph{Coreferential} ambiguity involves unclear pronoun or NP reference (e.g., ``\\ldots which must be approved by a supervisor'' where ``which'' could refer to the verification or the refund).\n\n\\paragraph{Specification layer.} These types arise from pragmatic or discourse-level gaps requiring domain knowledge to resolve.\n\\emph{Authorization scope} covers vague permission or role boundaries (e.g., ``authorized personnel may issue refunds'' without specifying which personnel).\n\\emph{Incompleteness} denotes missing conditions or boundary cases (e.g., a return policy silent on damaged items); we control for its information-addition confound via logistic regression (\\S\\ref{sec:results}).\n\\emph{Conditional precedence} involves conflicting rules without priority ordering (e.g., ``premium customers receive free shipping'' vs.\\ ``orders under \\$50 require a shipping fee'').\n\nThe layer distinction reflects a design hypothesis motivated by prior typologies \\citep{massey_ambiguity_re_2014,berry_kamsties_2004}: linguistic ambiguity can in principle be partially resolved through tool responses or world knowledge, while specification ambiguity has no external resolution channel. This hypothesis motivates systematic coverage across both sources; its empirical status is reported in \\S\\ref{sec:results} (C3, inconclusive).\n\n\\paragraph{Epistemological status.}\nOur taxonomy uses \\emph{ambiguity} as a policy specification umbrella term encompassing two epistemologically distinct operations. Five types involve \\emph{interpretation selection}: the text admits multiple readings and the agent commits to one. Incompleteness involves \\emph{information addition}: the text lacks necessary content entirely, and the agent confabulates it. This distinction manifests empirically: incompleteness is the only type unresponsive to prompt-based disambiguation ($-$3.4\\,pp, n.s.; \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}) because prompts cannot supply absent domain knowledge. We retain both under a single umbrella because agents process all six types identically---committing without flagging uncertainty---and the resulting violations produce comparable safety impact (\\S\\ref{sec:results}). This inclusive scope aligns with RE usage, where ambiguity and incompleteness frequently co-occur \\citep{dalpiaz_pinpointing_2018}.\n",

```json
      "totalLines": 42
    }
```

```text
1	\section*{Limitations}
2	\label{sec:limitations}
3	
4	Our ambiguity pairs are synthetically constructed, so the 34.0\,pp effect is an upper bound; the SOPBench replication ($+$12.7\,pp unweighted mean) provides a cross-benchmark lower bound. We evaluate seven API-accessible models across two customer-service domains at temperature $= 0$; a healthcare cross-domain pilot ($+$58.8\,pp, $n = 250$; Appendix~\ref{app:healthcare}) confirms the effect generalizes beyond customer service, though smaller models remain untested. Failure mode coding relies on LLM annotators ($\kappa = 0.52$; human $\kappa = 0.32$, rising to $0.41$ after collapsing the spurious-compliance/structural-misparse boundary; improved guidelines, prior round $0.15$), though the coder prompt excludes ambiguity type labels, preventing type--mechanism contamination. All experiments use temperature $= 0$, measuring each model's modal interpretation---the policy-relevant quantity agents will most likely commit to and the typical deployment setting for policy-governed agents. The matched-pair design partially mitigates temperature effects: any variance increase at higher temperatures applies to both conditions, so the treatment effect direction is unlikely to reverse. Moreover, the unambiguous-condition violation rate is already near floor (6.9\%), so temperature-induced variance would predominantly affect the ambiguous condition, preserving or widening the gap. A temperature $= 0.7$ pilot across three model families (GPT-4.1, Claude Sonnet~4.6, DeepSeek-V3; Appendix~\ref{app:temperature}) confirms that the treatment effect is robust (85--97\% clause-level agreement with greedy decoding), though the remaining two models and extreme-temperature regimes remain untested; the per-model pilot ($n = 50$ pairs) is powered to detect effects $\geq$24.6\,pp at 80\% power, so smaller attenuations at higher temperatures cannot be ruled out. Three additional scope limitations warrant disclosure: (1) three of the five judges share the OpenAI model family; however, GPT-5.4-judged and GPT-4.1-judged models show similar mean violation rates (38.0\% vs.\ 45.3\%, driven by DeepSeek's high base rate rather than judge leniency; cross-model judge $\kappa = 0.653$, McNemar $p = 0.606$), suggesting the 3:2 allocation does not systematically inflate convergence estimates; (2) authorization scope has the lowest per-type judge agreement ($\kappa = 0.385$, $n \approx 17$), so conclusions about this type carry wider uncertainty; (3) the coreferential type, whose variants differ only in referent specification, was excluded from both the mitigation and SOPBench replication analyses, limiting generalization for that type; and (4) the ICC analysis uses only within-family variation (GPT-4o/4o-mini), so the reported reliability bounds may not hold across architecturally diverse model pairs. Future work should extend to non-customer-service domains and collect per-episode human mechanism annotations to validate the type--mechanism association.
5	
6	\section*{Data and Code Availability}
7	The 300 clause pairs, evaluation pipeline, analysis scripts, and raw episode traces will be released upon publication at \url{https://github.com/anonymous-nlp-2026/policy_ambiguity_safety}.
8	
```

_source line 11335 · 2026-05-24T14:44:12.252Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_014Gommvppg73W8oXoiXRjgN`</summary>

```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	LLM-based agents increasingly operate under natural-language policy specifications that inevitably contain ambiguity, yet no prior work causally links ambiguity to safety violations. Using a matched-pair design on $\tau^2$-bench with 300 clause pairs spanning a six-type taxonomy evaluated across five models, we show that ambiguous clauses increase violations by $+$34.0 percentage points overall and $+$31.0\,pp for the five types whose manipulation is strictly disambiguating (incompleteness conflates ambiguity with information addition); cross-benchmark replication on SOPBench confirms directional consistency ($+$12.7\,pp unweighted mean; all five $\Delta > 0$), and a healthcare pilot extends the effect beyond customer service ($+$58.8\,pp). Error analysis reveals that agents almost never recognize ambiguity and converge on identical violations with strong permissive bias, showing that agreement-based safety monitoring is blind to systematic policy misinterpretation. These findings motivate clause-level policy auditing over type-level risk screening.
3	\end{abstract}
4	
```

</details>

Even with 30% random misclassification, the effect remains +13.5pp with tight CI entirely above zero.\n\n3. **Attenuation pattern**: As expected from measurement error theory, random misclassification in matched-pair designs attenuates effects toward zero by a factor of (1 - 2*error_rate). The empirical attenuation matches this: at 25% error, the effect shrinks by ~50% (18.1 -&gt; 9.2 for coreferential; 33.9 -&gt; 17.0 for all types), consistent with the theoretical factor of 1 - 2(0.25) = 0.50.&lt;/result>\n&lt;usage>&lt;total_tokens>36016&lt;/total_tokens>&lt;tool_uses>4&lt;/tool_uses>&lt;duration_ms>65668&lt;/duration_ms>&lt;/usage>\n&lt;/task-notification>",

```json
    "newString": "Under unambiguous conditions, the ambient convergent violation rate is 1.7\\% (5/300 clauses with $\\geq$3/5 model violations), confirming that the observed 41.3\\% under ambiguity reflects a $+$39.6\\,pp ambiguity-induced excess rather than ambient model correlation. Excluding incompleteness, the excess over the Poisson binomial independence baseline remains robust ($+$8.3\\,pp, 95\\% CI $[2.2, 14.3]$, $p < 0.005$; Appendix~\\ref{app:nocuity_sans_incomp}), confirming the phenomenon is not driven by information insufficiency alone.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Error Analysis \\emph{[Exploratory]}}\n\\label{sec:analysis}\n\nThe following failure mode taxonomy emerges empirically from LLM-coded traces, distinct from the input ambiguity taxonomy (\\S\\ref{sec:taxonomy}).\n\nWe analyze 614 violation traces across all five models, coded into five failure modes ($\\kappa = 0.52$ \\citep{cohen_1960}; Gwet's AC$_1$ $= 0.62$ \\citep{gwet_2014}); the coder prompt excludes ambiguity type labels. Type--mechanism mutual information (NMI $= 0.23$; $V = 0.45$, 95\\% CI $[0.41, 0.50]$; Appendix~\\ref{app:type_mechanism}) provides structural evidence against LLM-coding circularity.\\footnote{$V$ is computed on a $6\\times 5$ table vs.\\ $6\\times 2$ for severity; higher dimensionality makes $V = 0.45$ conservative.} Human mechanism coding yields $\\kappa = 0.32$, rising to $0.41$ after collapsing the spurious-compliance/structural-misparse boundary; human-coded $V = 0.40$ (mean of two annotators; collapsed $V = 0.42$; Appendix~\\ref{app:human_validation}) corroborates the LLM-coded association ($V = 0.45$), which remains a moderate upper bound. The higher LLM agreement ($\\kappa = 0.52$) than human ($0.32$) may reflect shared classification conventions---a meta-level parallel to convergent nocuity (\\S\\ref{sec:nocuity})---so these patterns are best interpreted as categorization tendencies.\n\n\\subsection{Type-Specific Failure Mechanisms}\n\nThree failure patterns emerge with $\\geq$3/5 cross-model consistency (Figure~\\ref{fig:type_mechanism}; Appendix~\\ref{app:failure_details}): \\emph{structural misparse} dominates scopal, conditional precedence, and coreferential types; \\emph{information gap-filling} dominates incompleteness and lexical; and \\emph{boundary violation} dominates authorization scope exclusively (69.2\\%, $p < 10^{-4}$).\n\n\\subsection{Disambiguation Neglect}\n\\label{sec:dn}\n\nAgents rarely recognize ambiguity: overall awareness is 4.6\\%, with GPT-4.1 and Claude at 10--11\\% while Qwen3 and DeepSeek show zero ($p = 2.2 \\times 10^{-9}$; Appendix~\\ref{app:awareness_breakdown}). We term this \\emph{disambiguation neglect}---extending ambiguity collapse \\citep{liu_ambiguity_2023,hu_ambiguity_2025} from the representational level to the behavioral level: agents act on collapsed readings without recognizing ambiguity, producing irreversible safety violations rather than mere accuracy degradation. Convergent nocuity (\\S\\ref{sec:nocuity}) further extends to the population level. A prompt-based mitigation (Appendix~\\ref{app:mitigation}) confirms type-dependent effectiveness: authorization scope ($-$18.0\\,pp), lexical ($-$8.9\\,pp), but not incompleteness ($-$3.4\\,pp, n.s.).\n\n\\subsection{Convergent Nocuity}\n\\label{sec:nocuity}\n\nWe adopt `nocuous' from \\citet{berry_kamsties_2004}, but inverted: our agents exhibit \\emph{convergent} rather than \\emph{divergent} errors---whereas divergent nocuity produces detectable disagreement, convergent nocuity renders errors invisible to agreement-based monitoring. Among ambiguous clauses, 41.3\\% trigger majority-convergent violations ($\\geq$3/5 models; 13.0\\% unanimous)---we term this \\emph{convergent nocuity}, invisible in agreement-based evaluation. Under an independence assumption (binomial model with each model's observed violation rate), the expected $\\geq$3/5 convergence rate is 32.4\\%; the observed 41.3\\% exceeds this baseline by $\\sim$9\\,pp, and the Jaccard bootstrap ($p < 0.0001$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_family}) confirms non-independence. These convergent violations exhibit a \\emph{permissive attractor}: 77.0\\% reflect permissive interpretation ($p \\approx 0$; Appendix~\\ref{app:attractor}). Cross-family violation co-occurrence (mean Jaccard $= 0.46$, $n = 9$ pairs; Figure~\\ref{fig:convergent_nocuity}) approaches within-family levels ($0.54$, $n = 1$); bootstrap CI for the difference ($\\Delta = 0.084$ [0.016, 0.153]; $p < 0.0001$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_family}) confirms statistical significance but modest effect size, indicating convergent violations reflect shared architectural biases rather than training-data overlap. Excluding incompleteness, the excess over the Poisson binomial independence baseline remains robust ($+$8.3\\,pp, 95\\% CI $[2.2, 14.3]$, $p < 0.005$; Appendix~\\ref{app:nocuity_sans_incomp}), confirming the phenomenon is not driven by information insufficiency alone.\n\n",
```

```json
    "originalFile": "\\section{Results}\n\\label{sec:results}\n\nWe organize results around three analyses: the binary ambiguity effect (C1), the type partial ordering (C2, including the inconclusive layer effect C3), and the model$\\times$type interaction (C4). C1 and C2 are primary supported claims; C3 (layer effect) is inconclusive; C4 (model$\\times$type interaction) is exploratory.\n\n\\subsection{Binary Ambiguity Effect (C1)}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_variance_decomposition.pdf}\n\\caption{Variance decomposition of violation rates (temperature $= 0$). Clause identity: 22.4\\% (0.4\\% between types); condition: 15.8\\%; model: 1.0\\%; residual: 60.8\\%. Clause wording---not type membership---is the primary risk factor among measured predictors.}\n\\label{fig:variance_decomposition}\n\\end{figure}\n\nAcross all five models and six types, ambiguous clauses increase violation rates by 34.0 pp (41.0\\% vs.\\ 6.9\\%, McNemar OR $= 15.1$ (based on $n = 580$ discordant pairs), 95\\% CI $[10.78, 21.17]$, $p < 10^{-105}$). All six types individually produce significant increases (all $p < 10^{-5}$) across both retail ($+$30.6\\,pp) and airline ($+$37.7\\,pp) domains (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_domain}), and all five models show large effects ($+25.7$ to $+39.0$ pp, all $p < 10^{-17}$). Perturbation controls confirm specificity to semantic ambiguity (\\S\\ref{sec:method}). Figure~\\ref{fig:variance_decomposition} decomposes violation-rate variance\\footnote{Type III SS; logistic regression (Appendix~\\ref{app:regression}) confirms concordant results (Tjur's $R^2 = 0.175$; \\citealp{tjur_2009}).}: clause identity explains 22.4\\% (0.4\\% between types, 22.0\\% within), condition 15.8\\%, model 1.0\\%, and residual 60.8\\% ($\\sim$15\\% stochastic, $\\sim$46\\% systematic; Appendix~\\ref{app:residual}). Excluding incompleteness ($+$49.2\\,pp, subject to information-addition confound; Appendix~\\ref{app:confound}), the effect is $+$31.0\\,pp (95\\% CI $[27.9, 34.1]$; Appendix~\\ref{app:sans_incompleteness}). The confound-minimized coreferential type ($\\Delta$token $\\approx 7.8$ words) still produces $+$18.7\\,pp, isolating the ambiguity-only contribution. $\\Delta_{\\text{token}}$ is a coarse proxy for information content; authorization scope exhibits similar token differences ($\\Delta = 36.1$ words) without the information-addition confound.\n\nReplication on two additional models confirms the effect across seven models ($\\Delta = +20.0$ to $+33.6$\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:model_expansion}); SOPBench~\\citep{li_sopbench_2025} confirms directional consistency ($+12.7$\\,pp unweighted mean; Appendix~\\ref{app:sopbench}). The taxonomy predicts failure mechanisms more strongly than violation rate (NMI $= 0.23$ vs.\\ $0.01$; $V = 0.45$ vs.\\ $0.14$ on tables of different dimensionality; Appendix~\\ref{app:type_mechanism}), making type-level analysis (C2--C4) informative for diagnosis.\n\n\\FloatBarrier\n\\subsection{Type Partial Ordering (C2)}\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Violation rates (\\%) under ambiguous policy clauses by type and model (5.4 = GPT-5.4, 4.1 = GPT-4.1, Cla.\\ = Claude, Qw3 = Qwen3, DS = DeepSeek). $\\Delta$pp: pooled difference (ambiguous $-$ unambiguous) with Newcombe 95\\% CIs. Cluster: TOST equivalence cluster at $\\pm 15$\\,pp---U = upper bound (separated at all margins), M = mid-tier (6/6 pairwise equivalent), L = lower (separates at $\\pm 12$\\,pp). Layer grouping (Spec./Ling.) did not survive correction (C3, inconclusive; \\S\\ref{sec:results}). All per-type $p < 10^{-5}$ (Fisher's exact).}\n\\label{tab:main_results}\n\\footnotesize\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3.8pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{llrrrrrrr@{\\hskip 6pt}cr}\n\\toprule\nLayer & Type & Pooled & 5.4 & 4.1 & Cla. & Qw3 & DS & $\\Delta$pp\\,[95\\% CI] & Cl. & $p$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{3}{*}{\\textcolor{speccolor}{Spec.}} & Incompleteness$^\\dagger$ & 53.6 & 46 & 58 & 48 & 62 & 54 & +49.2\\,[42.2, 55.6] & U & $<\\!10^{-37}$ \\\\\n & Auth.\\ Scope$^\\ddagger$ & 41.6 & 64 & 46 & 12 & 26 & 60 & +36.0\\,[29.1, 42.6] & M & $<\\!10^{-22}$ \\\\\n & Cond.\\ Prec. & 36.8 & 34 & 36 & 36 & 40 & 38 & +30.8\\,[24.0, 37.4] & M & $<\\!10^{-17}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{3}{*}{\\textcolor{lingcolor}{Ling.}} & Lexical & 42.8 & 42 & 34 & 32 & 52 & 54 & +35.2\\,[28.0, 42.0] & M & $<\\!10^{-20}$ \\\\\n & Scopal & 39.4 & 42 & 38 & 32 & 37 & 48 & +34.2\\,[27.4, 40.7] & M & $<\\!10^{-21}$ \\\\\n & Coreferential & 31.6 & 18 & 32 & 22 & 42 & 44 & +18.7\\,[11.5, 25.7] & L & $<\\!10^{-6}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{2}{l}{\\textbf{Overall}} & \\textbf{41.0} & 41 & 41 & 30 & 43 & 50 & \\textbf{+34.0}\\,[31.3, 36.9] & --- & $<\\!10^{-105}$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\n\\vspace{2pt}\n{\\footnotesize $\\dagger$\\,Subject to information-addition confound; see \\S\\ref{sec:results} and Appendix~\\ref{app:confound}. $\\ddagger$\\,Lowest inter-judge agreement ($\\kappa = 0.385$, per-type calibration $n \\approx 17$); $\\Delta$token$\\,= 36.1$ words, subject to information-addition confound analogous to incompleteness; see \\S\\ref{sec:results}. Excluding incompleteness: pooled $\\Delta = +31.0$\\,pp (95\\% CI $[27.9, 34.1]$).}\n\\end{table*}\n\nThe six types are not interchangeable. An omnibus $\\chi^2$ test on ambiguous-condition violation rates rejects the uniformity null ($\\chi^2 = 28.02$, $df = 5$, $p = 3.6 \\times 10^{-5}$, Cram\\'{e}r's $V = 0.137$). Table~\\ref{tab:tost_sensitivity} reveals a margin-dependent equivalence structure: three types achieve equivalence at $\\pm 10$\\,pp, five at $\\pm 12$\\,pp, and all six at $\\pm 15$\\,pp; we present the full sensitivity analysis without anchoring to a single margin. The NMI comparison ($0.23$ vs.\\ $0.01$) is threshold-independent. Table~\\ref{tab:main_results} and Figure~\\ref{fig:forest_plot} present the full breakdown.\n\nBootstrap clustering ($B = 10{,}000$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cluster}) confirms stable extremes across all margins. Cross-model concordance is moderate ($W = 0.398$, $p = 0.072$; Appendix~\\ref{app:kendall_w}). Authorization scope exhibits the widest cross-model range (12\\%--64\\%) and the lowest per-type judge agreement ($\\kappa = 0.385$); conclusions about this type carry wider uncertainty than the pooled estimates.\n\nCross-model judge validation ($\\rho = 0.71$--$0.89$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_judge}) confirms type-ranking stability when model composition is held constant; the low two-group concordance ($\\rho = 0.09$; Appendix~\\ref{app:judge_group_ranking}) reflects differential vulnerability to authorization scope$^\\ddagger$ (12\\%--64\\% range), not ranking instability. The partial ordering has robust extremes---incompleteness as upper bound and coreferential as lower bound, replicated across seven models and three judges---with model-composition-dependent mid-tier structure.\n\nIncompleteness ranks highest in four of five models ($\\geq$46\\% in all seven; Appendix~\\ref{app:model_expansion}); its 49.2\\,pp effect is an upper bound (Appendix~\\ref{app:confound}). C3 (layer grouping) did not survive Holm--Bonferroni ($p = 0.017$, threshold $0.0125$).\n\n\\begin{figure}[!ht]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_forest_plot.pdf}\n\\caption{Per-type effect sizes ($\\Delta$pp) with Newcombe 95\\% CIs (overall: $+$34.0\\,pp, OR $= 15.1$). Types form a partial ordering: incompleteness as upper bound (conditional on information-addition confound), coreferential as lower bound, and a mid-range equivalence cluster. Mid-tier ordering is margin-dependent (\\S\\ref{sec:results}).}\n\\label{fig:forest_plot}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\label{sec:c4}C4 (model$\\times$type interaction) is exploratory and driven solely by authorization scope; details in Appendix~\\ref{app:c4_sans_auth}.\n\n",
```

```json
    "originalFile": "\\begin{abstract}\nLLM-based agents increasingly operate under natural-language policy specifications that inevitably contain ambiguity, yet no prior work causally links ambiguity to safety violations. Using a matched-pair design on $\\tau^2$-bench with 300 clause pairs spanning a six-type taxonomy evaluated across five models, we show that ambiguous clauses increase violations by $+$34.0 percentage points overall and $+$31.0\\,pp for the five types whose manipulation is strictly disambiguating (incompleteness conflates ambiguity with information addition); cross-benchmark replication on SOPBench confirms directional consistency ($+$12.7\\,pp unweighted mean; all five $\\Delta > 0$), and a healthcare pilot extends the effect beyond customer service ($+$58.8\\,pp). Error analysis reveals that agents almost never recognize ambiguity and converge on identical violations with strong permissive bias, showing that agreement-based safety monitoring is blind to systematic policy misinterpretation. These findings motivate clause-level policy auditing over type-level risk screening.\n\\end{abstract}\n",
```

```text
50	Bootstrap clustering ($B = 10{,}000$; Appendix~\ref{app:cluster}) confirms stable extremes across all margins. Cross-model concordance is moderate ($W = 0.398$, $p = 0.072$; Appendix~\ref{app:kendall_w}). Authorization scope exhibits the widest cross-model range (12\%--64\%) and the lowest per-type judge agreement ($\kappa = 0.385$); conclusions about this type carry wider uncertainty than the pooled estimates.
51	
52	Cross-model judge validation ($\rho = 0.71$--$0.89$; Appendix~\ref{app:cross_judge}) confirms type-ranking stability when model composition is held constant; the low two-group concordance ($\rho = 0.09$; Appendix~\ref{app:judge_group_ranking}) reflects differential vulnerability to authorization scope$^\ddagger$ (12\%--64\% range), not ranking instability. The partial ordering has robust extremes---incompleteness as upper bound and coreferential as lower bound, replicated across seven models and three judges---with model-composition-dependent mid-tier structure.
53	
54	Incompleteness ranks highest in four of five models ($\geq$46\% in all seven; Appendix~\ref{app:model_expansion}); its 49.2\,pp effect is an upper bound (Appendix~\ref{app:confound}). C3 (layer grouping) did not survive Holm--Bonferroni ($p = 0.017$, threshold $0.0125$).
```

### Tool result

_source line 11388 · 2026-05-24T14:47:00.975Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_018yWB4gpVWPKqkPdqpt61Zj`</summary>

```text
15	Across all five models and six types, ambiguous clauses increase violation rates by 34.0 pp (41.0\% vs.\ 6.9\%, McNemar OR $= 15.1$ (based on $n = 580$ discordant pairs), 95\% CI $[10.78, 21.17]$, $p < 10^{-105}$). All six types individually produce significant increases (all $p < 10^{-5}$) across both retail ($+$30.6\,pp) and airline ($+$37.7\,pp) domains (Appendix~\ref{app:cross_domain}), and all five models show large effects ($+25.7$ to $+39.0$ pp, all $p < 10^{-17}$). Perturbation controls confirm specificity to semantic ambiguity (\S\ref{sec:method}). Figure~\ref{fig:variance_decomposition} decomposes violation-rate variance\footnote{Type III SS; logistic regression (Appendix~\ref{app:regression}) confirms concordant results (Tjur's $R^2 = 0.175$; \citealp{tjur_2009}).}: clause identity explains 22.4\% (0.4\% between types, 22.0\% within), condition 15.8\%, model 1.0\%, and residual 60.8\% ($\sim$15\% stochastic, $\sim$46\% systematic; Appendix~\ref{app:residual}). Excluding incompleteness ($+$49.2\,pp, subject to information-addition confound; Appendix~\ref{app:confound}), the effect is $+$31.0\,pp (95\% CI $[27.9, 34.1]$; Appendix~\ref{app:sans_incompleteness}). The confound-minimized coreferential type ($\Delta$token $\approx 7.8$ words) still produces $+$18.7\,pp, isolating the ambiguity-only contribution. $\Delta_{\text{token}}$ is a coarse proxy for information content; authorization scope exhibits similar token differences ($\Delta = 36.1$ words) without the information-addition confound.
16	
17	Replication on two additional models confirms the effect across seven models ($\Delta = +20.0$ to $+33.6$\,pp; Appendix~\ref{app:model_expansion}); SOPBench~\citep{li_sopbench_2025} confirms directional consistency ($+12.7$\,pp unweighted mean; Appendix~\ref{app:sopbench}). The taxonomy predicts failure mechanisms more strongly than violation rate (NMI $= 0.23$ vs.\ $0.01$; $V = 0.45$ vs.\ $0.14$ on tables of different dimensionality; Appendix~\ref{app:type_mechanism}), making type-level analysis (C2--C4) informative for diagnosis.
18	
19	\FloatBarrier
```

```json
    "originalFile": "\\section{Results}\n\\label{sec:results}\n\nWe organize results around three analyses: the binary ambiguity effect (C1), the type partial ordering (C2, including the inconclusive layer effect C3), and the model$\\times$type interaction (C4). C1 and C2 are primary supported claims; C3 (layer effect) is inconclusive; C4 (model$\\times$type interaction) is exploratory.\n\n\\subsection{Binary Ambiguity Effect (C1)}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_variance_decomposition.pdf}\n\\caption{Variance decomposition of violation rates (temperature $= 0$). Clause identity: 22.4\\% (0.4\\% between types); condition: 15.8\\%; model: 1.0\\%; residual: 60.8\\%. Clause wording---not type membership---is the primary risk factor among measured predictors.}\n\\label{fig:variance_decomposition}\n\\end{figure}\n\nAcross all five models and six types, ambiguous clauses increase violation rates by 34.0 pp (41.0\\% vs.\\ 6.9\\%, McNemar OR $= 15.1$ (based on $n = 580$ discordant pairs), 95\\% CI $[10.78, 21.17]$, $p < 10^{-105}$). All six types individually produce significant increases (all $p < 10^{-5}$) across both retail ($+$30.6\\,pp) and airline ($+$37.7\\,pp) domains (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_domain}), and all five models show large effects ($+25.7$ to $+39.0$ pp, all $p < 10^{-17}$). Perturbation controls confirm specificity to semantic ambiguity (\\S\\ref{sec:method}). Figure~\\ref{fig:variance_decomposition} decomposes violation-rate variance\\footnote{Type III SS; logistic regression (Appendix~\\ref{app:regression}) confirms concordant results (Tjur's $R^2 = 0.175$; \\citealp{tjur_2009}).}: clause identity explains 22.4\\% (0.4\\% between types, 22.0\\% within), condition 15.8\\%, model 1.0\\%, and residual 60.8\\% ($\\sim$15\\% stochastic, $\\sim$46\\% systematic; Appendix~\\ref{app:residual}). Excluding incompleteness ($+$49.2\\,pp, subject to information-addition confound; Appendix~\\ref{app:confound}), the effect is $+$31.0\\,pp (95\\% CI $[27.9, 34.1]$; Appendix~\\ref{app:sans_incompleteness}). The confound-minimized coreferential type ($\\Delta$token $\\approx 7.8$ words) still produces $+$18.7\\,pp, isolating the ambiguity-only contribution. $\\Delta_{\\text{token}}$ is a coarse proxy for information content; authorization scope exhibits similar token differences ($\\Delta = 36.1$ words) without the information-addition confound.\n\nReplication on two additional models confirms the effect across seven models ($\\Delta = +20.0$ to $+33.6$\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:model_expansion}); SOPBench~\\citep{li_sopbench_2025} confirms directional consistency ($+12.7$\\,pp unweighted mean; Appendix~\\ref{app:sopbench}). The taxonomy predicts failure mechanisms more strongly than violation rate (NMI $= 0.23$ vs.\\ $0.01$; $V = 0.45$ vs.\\ $0.14$ on tables of different dimensionality; Appendix~\\ref{app:type_mechanism}), making type-level analysis (C2--C4) informative for diagnosis.\n\n\\FloatBarrier\n\\subsection{Type Partial Ordering (C2)}\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Violation rates (\\%) under ambiguous policy clauses by type and model (5.4 = GPT-5.4, 4.1 = GPT-4.1, Cla.\\ = Claude, Qw3 = Qwen3, DS = DeepSeek). $\\Delta$pp: pooled difference (ambiguous $-$ unambiguous) with Newcombe 95\\% CIs. Cluster: TOST equivalence cluster at $\\pm 15$\\,pp---U = upper bound (separated at all margins), M = mid-tier (6/6 pairwise equivalent), L = lower (separates at $\\pm 12$\\,pp). Layer grouping (Spec./Ling.) did not survive correction (C3, inconclusive; \\S\\ref{sec:results}). All per-type $p < 10^{-5}$ (Fisher's exact).}\n\\label{tab:main_results}\n\\footnotesize\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3.8pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{llrrrrrrr@{\\hskip 6pt}cr}\n\\toprule\nLayer & Type & Pooled & 5.4 & 4.1 & Cla. & Qw3 & DS & $\\Delta$pp\\,[95\\% CI] & Cl. & $p$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{3}{*}{\\textcolor{speccolor}{Spec.}} & Incompleteness$^\\dagger$ & 53.6 & 46 & 58 & 48 & 62 & 54 & +49.2\\,[42.2, 55.6] & U & $<\\!10^{-37}$ \\\\\n & Auth.\\ Scope$^\\ddagger$ & 41.6 & 64 & 46 & 12 & 26 & 60 & +36.0\\,[29.1, 42.6] & M & $<\\!10^{-22}$ \\\\\n & Cond.\\ Prec. & 36.8 & 34 & 36 & 36 & 40 & 38 & +30.8\\,[24.0, 37.4] & M & $<\\!10^{-17}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{3}{*}{\\textcolor{lingcolor}{Ling.}} & Lexical & 42.8 & 42 & 34 & 32 & 52 & 54 & +35.2\\,[28.0, 42.0] & M & $<\\!10^{-20}$ \\\\\n & Scopal & 39.4 & 42 & 38 & 32 & 37 & 48 & +34.2\\,[27.4, 40.7] & M & $<\\!10^{-21}$ \\\\\n & Coreferential & 31.6 & 18 & 32 & 22 & 42 & 44 & +18.7\\,[11.5, 25.7] & L & $<\\!10^{-6}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{2}{l}{\\textbf{Overall}} & \\textbf{41.0} & 41 & 41 & 30 & 43 & 50 & \\textbf{+34.0}\\,[31.3, 36.9] & --- & $<\\!10^{-105}$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\n\\vspace{2pt}\n{\\footnotesize $\\dagger$\\,Subject to information-addition confound; see \\S\\ref{sec:results} and Appendix~\\ref{app:confound}. $\\ddagger$\\,Lowest inter-judge agreement ($\\kappa = 0.385$, per-type calibration $n \\approx 17$); $\\Delta$token$\\,= 36.1$ words, subject to information-addition confound analogous to incompleteness; see \\S\\ref{sec:results}. Excluding incompleteness: pooled $\\Delta = +31.0$\\,pp (95\\% CI $[27.9, 34.1]$).}\n\\end{table*}\n\nThe six types are not interchangeable. An omnibus $\\chi^2$ test on ambiguous-condition violation rates rejects the uniformity null ($\\chi^2 = 28.02$, $df = 5$, $p = 3.6 \\times 10^{-5}$, Cram\\'{e}r's $V = 0.137$). Table~\\ref{tab:tost_sensitivity} reveals a margin-dependent equivalence structure: three types achieve equivalence at $\\pm 10$\\,pp, five at $\\pm 12$\\,pp, and all six at $\\pm 15$\\,pp; we present the full sensitivity analysis without anchoring to a single margin. The resulting partial ordering is exploratory and contingent on the $\\pm 15$\\,pp SESOI choice (Table~\\ref{tab:tost_sensitivity} sensitivity columns show instability below $\\pm 12$\\,pp). The NMI comparison ($0.23$ vs.\\ $0.01$) is threshold-independent. Table~\\ref{tab:main_results} and Figure~\\ref{fig:forest_plot} present the full breakdown.\n\nBootstrap clustering ($B = 10{,}000$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cluster}) confirms stable extremes across all margins. Cross-model concordance is moderate ($W = 0.398$, $p = 0.072$; Appendix~\\ref{app:kendall_w}). Authorization scope exhibits the widest cross-model range (12\\%--64\\%) and the lowest per-type judge agreement ($\\kappa = 0.385$); conclusions about this type carry wider uncertainty than the pooled estimates.\n\nCross-model judge validation ($\\rho = 0.71$--$0.89$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_judge}) confirms type-ranking stability when model composition is held constant; the low two-group concordance ($\\rho = 0.09$; Appendix~\\ref{app:judge_group_ranking}) reflects differential vulnerability to authorization scope$^\\ddagger$ (12\\%--64\\% range), not ranking instability. The partial ordering has robust extremes---incompleteness as upper bound and coreferential as lower bound, replicated across seven models and three judges---with model-composition-dependent mid-tier structure.\n\nIncompleteness ranks highest in four of five models ($\\geq$46\\% in all seven; Appendix~\\ref{app:model_expansion}); its 49.2\\,pp effect is an upper bound (Appendix~\\ref{app:confound}). C3 (layer grouping) did not survive Holm--Bonferroni ($p = 0.017$, threshold $0.0125$).\n\n\\begin{figure}[!ht]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_forest_plot.pdf}\n\\caption{Per-type effect sizes ($\\Delta$pp) with Newcombe 95\\% CIs (overall: $+$34.0\\,pp, OR $= 15.1$). ```text
```
```text
1	\section{Discussion}
2	\label{sec:discussion}
3	
4	\paragraph{Disambiguation neglect and prompt-level safety.}
5	The 4.6\% awareness rate implies prompt-level safety instructions are insufficient. Authorization scope responds to explicit disambiguation ($-$18.0\,pp), but incompleteness does not ($-$3.4\,pp, n.s.) because missing information cannot be recovered from the prompt alone (Appendix~\ref{app:mitigation}).
6	
7	\paragraph{Convergent nocuity and agreement-based evaluation.}
8	Convergent nocuity operates at two levels. At the \emph{practical} level, 41.3\% of ambiguous clauses trigger majority-convergent violations---regardless of whether this convergence arises from independent high violation rates or shared bias, multi-agent consensus is unreliable as a safety proxy for these clauses. At the \emph{mechanism} level, the observed convergence exceeds the Poisson binomial independence baseline by $+$8.3\,pp (95\% CI $[2.2, 14.3]$; excluding incompleteness; Appendix~\ref{app:nocuity_sans_incomp}), and cross-family Jaccard ($0.46$; Figure~\ref{fig:convergent_nocuity}) approaches within-family levels ($0.54$), indicating shared architectural biases beyond independent vulnerability. The practical implication is immediate: agreement-based monitoring cannot detect the most systematic failures, because the failures themselves are convergent.
9	
10	\paragraph{Practical implications.}
11	The type--mechanism dissociation maps types to targeted mitigations: numerical thresholds for authorization scope ($-$36.0\,pp), structural disambiguation for scopal, and default-value specification for incompleteness. Effect magnitudes range from $+$12.7\,pp under naturalistic conditions (SOPBench) to $+$58.8\,pp in a healthcare pilot, consistent with the upper/lower bound framing in which $\tau^2$-bench ($+$34.0\,pp) provides a controlled upper bound.
12	
```

```json
    "originalFile": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Disambiguation neglect and prompt-level safety.}\nThe 4.6\\% awareness rate implies prompt-level safety instructions are insufficient. At the \\emph{practical} level, 41.3\\% of ambiguous clauses trigger majority-convergent violations---regardless of whether this convergence arises from independent high violation rates or shared bias, multi-agent consensus is unreliable as a safety proxy for these clauses. At the \\emph{mechanism} level, the observed convergence exceeds the Poisson binomial independence baseline by $+$8.3\\,pp (95\\% CI $[2.2, 14.3]$; excluding incompleteness; Appendix~\\ref{app:nocuity_sans_incomp}), and cross-family Jaccard ($0.46$; Figure~\\ref{fig:convergent_nocuity}) approaches within-family levels ($0.54$), indicating shared architectural biases beyond independent vulnerability. {
```

```json
  "parentUuid": "b1567956-cf4d-4d04-80e3-576823c5c167",

  "promptId": "927350ef-d4d1-4783-a1cd-8647bc7f710e",

    "oldString": "the SOPBench replication ($+$12.7\\,pp unweighted mean) provides a cross-benchmark lower bound.",
    "newString": "the SOPBench evaluation ($+$12.7\\,pp unweighted mean) provides a cross-benchmark lower bound.",
    "originalFile": "\\section*{Limitations}\n\\label{sec:limitations}\n\nOur ambiguity pairs are synthetically constructed, so the 34.0\\,pp effect is an upper bound; the SOPBench replication ($+$12.7\\,pp unweighted mean) provides a cross-benchmark lower bound. We evaluate seven API-accessible models across two customer-service domains at temperature $= 0$; a healthcare cross-domain pilot ($+$58.8\\,pp, $n = 250$; Appendix~\\ref{app:healthcare}) confirms the effect generalizes beyond customer service, though smaller models remain untested. Failure mode coding relies on LLM annotators ($\\kappa = 0.52$; human $\\kappa = 0.32$, rising to $0.41$ after collapsing the spurious-compliance/structural-misparse boundary; improved guidelines, prior round $0.15$), though the coder prompt excludes ambiguity type labels, preventing type--mechanism contamination. All experiments use temperature $= 0$, measuring each model's modal interpretation---the policy-relevant quantity agents will most likely commit to and the typical deployment setting for policy-governed agents. The matched-pair design partially mitigates temperature effects: any variance increase at higher temperatures applies to both conditions, so the treatment effect direction is unlikely to reverse. Moreover, the unambiguous-condition violation rate is already near floor (6.9\\%), so temperature-induced variance would predominantly affect the ambiguous condition, preserving or widening the gap. A temperature $= 0.7$ pilot across three model families (GPT-4.1, Claude Sonnet~4.6, DeepSeek-V3; Appendix~\\ref{app:temperature}) confirms that the treatment effect is robust (85--97\\% clause-level agreement with greedy decoding), though the remaining two models and extreme-temperature regimes remain untested; the per-model pilot ($n = 50$ pairs) is powered to detect effects $\\geq$24.6\\,pp at 80\\% power, so smaller attenuations at higher temperatures cannot be ruled out. Three additional scope limitations warrant disclosure: (1) three of the five judges share the OpenAI model family; however, GPT-5.4-judged and GPT-4.1-judged models show similar mean violation rates (38.0\\% vs.\\ 45.3\\%, driven by DeepSeek's high base rate rather than judge leniency; cross-model judge $\\kappa = 0.653$, McNemar $p = 0.606$), suggesting the 3:2 allocation does not systematically inflate convergence estimates; (2) authorization scope has the lowest per-type judge agreement ($\\kappa = 0.385$, $n \\approx 17$), so conclusions about this type carry wider uncertainty; (3) the coreferential type, whose variants differ only in referent specification, was excluded from both the mitigation and SOPBench replication analyses, limiting generalization for that type; and (4) the ICC analysis uses only within-family variation (GPT-4o/4o-mini), so the reported reliability bounds may not hold across architecturally diverse model pairs. Future work should extend to non-customer-service domains and collect per-episode human mechanism annotations to validate the type--mechanism association.\n\n\\section*{Data and Code Availability}\nThe 300 clause pairs, evaluation pipeline, analysis scripts, and raw episode traces will be released upon publication at \\url{https://github.com/anonymous-nlp-2026/policy_ambiguity_safety}.\n",
    "structuredPatch": [
      {
        "oldStart": 1,
        "oldLines": 7,
        "newStart": 1,
        "newLines": 7,
        "lines": [
          " \\section*{Limitations}",
          " \\label{sec:limitations}",
          " ",
          "-Our ambiguity pairs are synthetically constructed, so the 34.0\\,pp effect is an upper bound; the SOPBench replication ($+$12.7\\,pp unweighted mean) provides a cross-benchmark lower bound. We evaluate seven API-accessible models across two customer-service domains at temperature $= 0$; a healthcare cross-domain pilot ($+$58.8\\,pp, $n = 250$; Appendix~\\ref{app:healthcare}) confirms the effect generalizes beyond customer service, though smaller models remain untested. Failure mode coding relies on LLM annotators ($\\kappa = 0.52$; human $\\kappa = 0.32$, rising to $0.41$ after collapsing the spurious-compliance/structural-misparse boundary; improved guidelines, prior round $0.15$), though the coder prompt excludes ambiguity type labels, preventing type--mechanism contamination. All experiments use temperature $= 0$, measuring each model's modal interpretation---the policy-relevant quantity agents will most likely commit to and the typical deployment setting for policy-governed agents. The matched-pair design partially mitigates temperature effects: any variance increase at higher temperatures applies to both conditions, so the treatment effect direction is unlikely to reverse. Moreover, the unambiguous-condition violation rate is already near floor (6.9\\%), so temperature-induced variance would predominantly affect the ambiguous condition, preserving or widening the gap. A temperature $= 0.7$ pilot across three model families (GPT-4.1, Claude Sonnet~4.6, DeepSeek-V3; Appendix~\\ref{app:temperature}) confirms that the treatment effect is robust (85--97\\% clause-level agreement with greedy decoding), though the remaining two models and extreme-temperature regimes remain untested; the per-model pilot ($n = 50$ pairs) is powered to detect effects $\\geq$24.6\\,pp at 80\\% power, so smaller attenuations at higher temperatures cannot be ruled out. Three additional scope limitations warrant disclosure: (1) three of the five judges share the OpenAI model family; however, GPT-5.4-judged and GPT-4.1-judged models show similar mean violation rates (38.0\\% vs.\\ 45.3\\%, driven by DeepSeek's high base rate rather than judge leniency; cross-model judge $\\kappa = 0.653$, McNemar $p = 0.606$), suggesting the 3:2 allocation does not systematically inflate convergence estimates; (2) authorization scope has the lowest per-type judge agreement ($\\kappa = 0.385$, $n \\approx 17$), so conclusions about this type carry wider uncertainty; (3) the coreferential type, whose variants differ only in referent specification, was excluded from both the mitigation and SOPBench replication analyses, limiting generalization for that type; and (4) the ICC analysis uses only within-family variation (GPT-4o/4o-mini), so the reported reliability bounds may not hold across architecturally diverse model pairs. Future work should extend to non-customer-service domains and collect per-episode human mechanism annotations to validate the type--mechanism association.",
          "+Our ambiguity pairs are synthetically constructed, so the 34.0\\,pp effect is an upper bound; the SOPBench evaluation ($+$12.7\\,pp unweighted mean) provides a cross-benchmark lower bound. Failure mode coding relies on LLM annotators ($\\kappa = 0.52$; human $\\kappa = 0.32$, rising to $0.41$ after collapsing the spurious-compliance/structural-misparse boundary; improved guidelines, prior round $0.15$), though the coder prompt excludes ambiguity type labels, preventing type--mechanism contamination. All experiments use temperature $= 0$, measuring each model's modal interpretation---the policy-relevant quantity agents will most likely commit to and the typical deployment setting for policy-governed agents. The matched-pair design partially mitigates temperature effects: any variance increase at higher temperatures applies to both conditions, so the treatment effect direction is unlikely to reverse. Moreover, the unambiguous-condition violation rate is already near floor (6.9\\%), so temperature-induced variance would predominantly affect the ambiguous condition, preserving or widening the gap. 19	Our results reveal a \emph{type--mechanism dissociation}: policy ambiguity consistently increases violations ($+34.0$\,pp under synthetic conditions; $+31.0$\,pp excluding incompleteness), but ambiguity \emph{type} predicts \emph{how} agents fail, not \emph{whether} they fail. Clause wording explains 22.4\% of violation variance while type membership explains only 0.4\%; yet type predicts failure mechanism substantially more strongly than violation rate (NMI $= 0.23$ vs.\ $0.01$; Cram\'{e}r's $V = 0.45$ vs.\ $0.14$ on tables of different dimensionality; Appendix~\ref{app:type_mechanism}). This dissociation suggests the taxonomy may function as a diagnostic instrument rather than a risk classifier (\S\ref{sec:discussion}). Incompleteness is the highest-risk type (46--62\%); the remaining types form a partial ordering with robust extremes but margin-dependent mid-tier structure (Table~\ref{tab:tost_sensitivity}).
```
```text
20	
21	Our contributions are: (1)~the first matched-pair causal study establishing that policy ambiguity increases violations by $+$34.0\,pp ($+$31.0\,pp excluding incompleteness; $+$18.7\,pp for the confound-minimized coreferential type), replicated on SOPBench ($+$12.7\,pp unweighted mean; all five $\Delta > 0$), with clause wording---not type membership---as the primary risk factor among measured predictors (22.4\% vs.\ 0.4\% of variance; \S\ref{sec:results}); and (2)~\emph{convergent nocuity}---a failure mode in which 41.3\% of ambiguous clauses trigger majority-convergent violations across architecturally diverse models (Jaccard $= 0.46$ cross-family vs.\ $0.54$ within-family), with 77\% permissive bias, rendering agreement-based monitoring blind to systematic failures (\S\ref{sec:analysis}). An exploratory type--mechanism dissociation is reported in \S\ref{sec:analysis}.
22
```
