    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nA customer asks to return a customized item within the 30-day window. The agent consults the store policy, which states that ``items may be returned within 30 days.'' The word ``items'' scopes over all product categories, including custom orders that are normally final sale. The agent grants a full refund and arranges free return shipping. One word of scopal ambiguity costs the retailer \\$200.\n\nLLM-based agents increasingly operate under natural-language policy specifications that govern their permitted actions. These specifications inevitably contain ambiguity---a pilot audit of 407 clauses from 24 production policy documents finds a majority of clauses contain at least one ambiguity type (55--87\\%, depending on union vs.\\ intersection of two judges; $\\kappa = 0.30$; Appendix~\\ref{app:prevalence})---a property studied for decades in requirements engineering (RE) and computational linguistics. \\citet{berry_kamsties_2004} distinguish between \\emph{nocuous} ambiguity, where different stakeholders arrive at different interpretations, and \\emph{innocuous} ambiguity, where shared context ensures convergent interpretation. We document a distinct failure mode that breaks this assumption: LLM agents \\emph{converge} on the same incorrect interpretation, making ambiguity harmful through systematic, undetectable error rather than inconsistency (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}). We term this \\emph{convergent nocuity}: Berry's criterion---predicated on divergent interpretations---does not extend to LLM agents, where convergence reflects shared training biases rather than shared domain knowledge, and errors are invisible to agreement-based monitoring.\n\nLLM agents lack these disambiguation channels. They cannot consult colleagues, rarely escalate to users, and must resolve ambiguity in real time from the text alone. This raises a question that has not been empirically addressed: does policy ambiguity cause safety violations in LLM agents, and if so, does the \\emph{type} of ambiguity predict the severity of the violation? Prior work classifies ambiguity types in policies \\citep{drip_r_2026,li_ambiguity_taxonomy_2024} or measures policy understanding quality \\citep{policybank_2026}, but no existing study provides a causal, type-granularity mapping from ambiguity to safety violations.\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{figures/fig1_overview.pdf}\n\\caption{Experimental overview. (a)~Six-type ambiguity taxonomy (two layers) with 300 matched clause pairs. (b)~Matched-pair causal design: each clause pair is injected into $\\tau^2$-bench policy documents; five models interact under both ambiguous and unambiguous conditions across 2,997 episodes. (c)~Cross-model judge evaluation classifies violation severity; key findings show a $+$34.0\\,pp violation increase from ambiguity and convergent nocuity across models.}\n\\label{fig:overview}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nWe address this gap with a matched-pair causal study on $\\tau^2$-bench \\citep{tau2_bench_2025}, a dual-control conversational agent benchmark. Using a six-type taxonomy organized into two layers---linguistic and specification (\\S\\ref{sec:taxonomy})---we construct 300 ambiguous/unambiguous clause pairs and evaluate five architecturally diverse models across 2,997 episodes.\n\nOur results reveal a \\emph{type--mechanism dissociation}: policy ambiguity consistently increases violations ($+34.0$\\,pp under synthetic conditions; $+31.0$\\,pp excluding incompleteness), but ambiguity \\emph{type} predicts \\emph{how} agents fail, not \\emph{whether} they fail. Clause wording explains 22.4\\% of violation variance while type membership explains only 0.4\\%; yet type predicts failure mechanism substantially more strongly than violation rate (NMI $= 0.23$ vs.\\ $0.01$; Cram\\'{e}r's $V = 0.45$ vs.\\ $0.14$ on tables of different dimensionality; Appendix~\\ref{app:type_mechanism}). This dissociation suggests the taxonomy may function as a diagnostic instrument rather than a risk classifier (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}). Incompleteness is the highest-risk type (46--62\\%); the remaining types form a partial ordering with robust extremes but margin-dependent mid-tier structure (Table~\\ref{tab:tost_sensitivity}).\n\nOur contributions are: (1)~the first matched-pair causal study establishing that policy ambiguity increases violations by $+$34.0\\,pp ($+$31.0\\,pp excluding incompleteness; $+$18.7\\,pp for the confound-minimized coreferential type), replicated on SOPBench ($+$12.7\\,pp unweighted mean; all five $\\Delta > 0$), with clause wording---not type membership---as the primary risk factor among measured predictors (22.4\\% vs.\\ 0.4\\% of variance; \\S\\ref{sec:results}); and (2)~\\emph{convergent nocuity}---a failure mode in which 41.3\\% of ambiguous clauses trigger majority-convergent violations across architecturally diverse models (Jaccard $= 0.46$ cross-family vs.\\ $0.54$ within-family), with 77\\% permissive bias, rendering agreement-based monitoring blind to systematic failures (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}). An exploratory type--mechanism dissociation is reported in \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}.\n",

An exploratory type--mechanism dissociation is reported in \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}.",
          "+Our contributions are: (1)~the first matched-pair causal study establishing that policy ambiguity increases violations by $+$34.0\\,pp ($+$31.0\\,pp excluding incompleteness; $+$18.7\\,pp for the confound-minimized coreferential type), with convergent evidence on SOPBench ($+$12.7\\,pp unweighted mean; all five $\\Delta > 0$), with clause wording---not type membership---as the primary risk factor among measured predictors (22.4\\% vs.\\ 0.4\\% of variance; \\S\\ref{sec:results}); and (2)~\\emph{convergent nocuity}---a failure mode in which 41.3\\% of ambiguous clauses trigger majority-convergent violations across architecturally diverse models (Jaccard $= 0.46$ cross-family vs.\\ $0.54$ within-family), with 77\\% permissive bias, rendering agreement-based monitoring blind to systematic failures (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}). An exploratory type--mechanism dissociation is reported in \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}."
        ]

```text
590	\end{table}
591	
592	\section{Cross-Benchmark Replication (SOPBench)}
593	\label{app:sopbench}
594	
595	To assess generalizability beyond $\tau^2$-bench, we replicate the core ambiguity effect (C1) on SOPBench \citep{li_sopbench_2025}, a procedural compliance benchmark comprising 47 negative-task pairs across four domains (banking, healthcare, retail, administration). Each pair consists of an ambiguous and matched unambiguous standard operating procedure. We evaluate five models using the \texttt{constraint\_not\_violated} metric, which measures whether the agent respects policy constraints---analogous to our violation rate but operating at the constraint rather than episode level.
596	
597	\paragraph{Results.} Table~\ref{tab:sopbench} reports per-model violation rates. All five models show higher violation rates under ambiguous SOPs (unweighted mean $\Delta = +12.7$\,pp), confirming directional consistency with the $\tau^2$-bench main effect ($+34.0$\,pp). Three models reach individual significance at $p < 0.01$; GPT-5.4 is borderline ($p = 0.052$, two-sided). DeepSeek-V3 shows the smallest effect ($+5.3$\,pp, $p = 0.16$), attributable to its elevated unambiguous baseline (43.3\%), which compresses the ambiguity-specific delta.
598	
599	\paragraph{Effect size difference.} The smaller absolute effect on SOPBench likely reflects three systematic differences: (1)~SOPBench uses constraint-level rather than episode-level violation measurement, yielding finer-grained rates; (2)~SOPBench's negative tasks are designed to test constraint boundaries, creating higher baseline violation rates even under unambiguous SOPs (mean unambiguous rate: 22.7\% vs.\ $\tau^2$-bench 6.9\%); (3)~task structure differences (single-turn constraint checks vs.\ multi-turn customer service episodes). The key replication signal is directional consistency across all five models rather than absolute magnitude match.
600	
601	\paragraph{Structured attribution.} Three structural differences between $\tau^2$-bench and SOPBench contribute to the 63\% effect compression: (1)~\emph{measurement grain}---SOPBench evaluates individual constraints rather than full episodes, yielding finer-grained but smaller deltas; (2)~\emph{baseline rate compression}---SOPBench's higher unambiguous violation rate (22.7\% vs.\ 6.9\%) compresses the ambiguity-specific increment (ceiling effect); (3)~\emph{interaction depth}---single-turn constraint checks vs.\ multi-turn customer-service dialogues, where ambiguity effects may accumulate across turns. Each factor independently predicts effect attenuation without undermining directional consistency.
602	
603	\begin{table}[h]
604	\centering
605	\small
606	\caption{SOPBench cross-benchmark replication. All five models show directionally consistent ambiguity effects ($\Delta > 0$). $^\dagger$Power-expanded from $n = 139$. $p$: Fisher's exact, two-sided.}
607	\label{tab:sopbench}
608	\setlength{\tabcolsep}{4pt}
609	\begin{tabular}{lccccr}
```

All five models show higher violation rates under ambiguous SOPs (unweighted mean $\\Delta = +12.7$\\,pp), confirming directional consistency with the $\\tau^2$-bench main effect ($+34.0$\\,pp). Three models reach individual significance at $p < 0.01$; GPT-5.4 is borderline ($p = 0.052$, two-sided). DeepSeek-V3 shows the smallest effect ($+5.3$\\,pp, $p = 0.16$), attributable to its elevated unambiguous baseline (43.3\\%), which compresses the ambiguity-specific delta.",

The key replication signal is directional consistency across all five models rather than absolute magnitude match.",

          " \\paragraph{Structured attribution.} Three structural differences between $\\tau^2$-bench and SOPBench contribute to the 63\\% effect compression: (1)~\\emph{measurement grain}---SOPBench evaluates individual constraints rather than full episodes, yielding finer-grained but smaller deltas; (2)~\\emph{baseline rate compression}---SOPBench's higher unambiguous violation rate (22.7\\% vs.\\ 6.9\\%) compresses the ambiguity-specific increment (ceiling effect); (3)~\\emph{interaction depth}---single-turn constraint checks vs.\\ multi-turn customer-service dialogues, where ambiguity effects may accumulate across turns. {

  "parentUuid": "ffb49363-17ce-4fd9-9aca-f87cea0e66e0",

  "promptId": "927350ef-d4d1-4783-a1cd-8647bc7f710e",

    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-be-idea-gen-0519-4/projects/policy_ambiguity_safety/docs/paper/sections/limitations.tex",
    "oldString": "Future work should extend to non-customer-service domains and collect per-episode human mechanism annotations to validate the type--mechanism association.",
    "newString": "All evaluated models are frontier-class ($\\geq$100B parameters or equivalent capability tier); generalization to smaller open-source models (7B--70B) remains untested. Future work should extend to non-customer-service domains and collect per-episode human mechanism annotations to validate the type--mechanism association.",
    "originalFile": "\\section*{Limitations}\n\\label{sec:limitations}\n\nOur ambiguity pairs are synthetically constructed, so the 34.0\\,pp effect is an upper bound; the SOPBench evaluation ($+$12.7\\,pp unweighted mean) provides a cross-benchmark lower bound. We evaluate seven API-accessible models across two customer-service domains at temperature $= 0$; a healthcare cross-domain pilot ($+$58.8\\,pp, $n = 250$; Appendix~\\ref{app:healthcare}) confirms the effect generalizes beyond customer service, though smaller models remain untested. Failure mode coding relies on LLM annotators ($\\kappa = 0.52$; human $\\kappa = 0.32$, rising to $0.41$ after collapsing the spurious-compliance/structural-misparse boundary; improved guidelines, prior round $0.15$), though the coder prompt excludes ambiguity type labels, preventing type--mechanism contamination. All experiments use temperature $= 0$, measuring each model's modal interpretation---the policy-relevant quantity agents will most likely commit to and the typical deployment setting for policy-governed agents. The matched-pair design partially mitigates temperature effects: any variance increase at higher temperatures applies to both conditions, so the treatment effect direction is unlikely to reverse. Moreover, the unambiguous-condition violation rate is already near floor (6.9\\%), so temperature-induced variance would predominantly affect the ambiguous condition, preserving or widening the gap. A temperature $= 0.7$ pilot across three model families (GPT-4.1, Claude Sonnet~4.6, DeepSeek-V3; Appendix~\\ref{app:temperature}) confirms that the treatment effect is robust (85--97\\% clause-level agreement with greedy decoding), though the remaining two models and extreme-temperature regimes remain untested; the per-model pilot ($n = 50$ pairs) is powered to detect effects $\\geq$24.6\\,pp at 80\\% power, so smaller attenuations at higher temperatures cannot be ruled out. Three additional scope limitations warrant disclosure: (1) three of the five judges share the OpenAI model family; however, GPT-5.4-judged and GPT-4.1-judged models show similar mean violation rates (38.0\\% vs.\\ 45.3\\%, driven by DeepSeek's high base rate rather than judge leniency; cross-model judge $\\kappa = 0.653$, McNemar $p = 0.606$), suggesting the 3:2 allocation does not systematically inflate convergence estimates; (2) authorization scope has the lowest per-type judge agreement ($\\kappa = 0.385$, $n \\approx 17$), so conclusions about this type carry wider uncertainty; (3) the coreferential type, whose variants differ only in referent specification, was excluded from both the mitigation and SOPBench replication analyses, limiting generalization for that type; and (4) the ICC analysis uses only within-family variation (GPT-4o/4o-mini), so the reported reliability bounds may not hold across architecturally diverse model pairs. Future work should extend to non-customer-service domains and collect per-episode human mechanism annotations to validate the type--mechanism association.\n\n\\section*{Data and Code Availability}\nThe 300 clause pairs, evaluation pipeline, analysis scripts, and raw episode traces will be released upon publication at \\url{https://github.com/anonymous-nlp-2026/policy_ambiguity_safety}.\n",
    "structuredPatch": [
      {
        "oldStart": 1,
        "oldLines": 7,
        "newStart": 1,
        "newLines": 7,
        "lines": [
          " \\section*{Limitations}",
          " \\label{sec:limitations}",
          " ",
          "-Our ambiguity pairs are synthetically constructed, so the 34.0\\,pp effect is an upper bound; the SOPBench evaluation ($+$12.7\\,pp unweighted mean) provides a cross-benchmark lower bound. We evaluate seven API-accessible models across two customer-service domains at temperature $= 0$; a healthcare cross-domain pilot ($+$58.8\\,pp, $n = 250$; Appendix~\\ref{app:healthcare}) confirms the effect generalizes beyond customer service, though smaller models remain untested. Failure mode coding relies on LLM annotators ($\\kappa = 0.52$; human $\\kappa = 0.32$, rising to $0.41$ after collapsing the spurious-compliance/structural-misparse boundary; improved guidelines, prior round $0.15$), though the coder prompt excludes ambiguity type labels, preventing type--mechanism contamination. All experiments use temperature $= 0$, measuring each model's modal interpretation---the policy-relevant quantity agents will most likely commit to and the typical deployment setting for policy-governed agents. The matched-pair design partially mitigates temperature effects: any variance increase at higher temperatures applies to both conditions, so the treatment effect direction is unlikely to reverse. Moreover, the unambiguous-condition violation rate is already near floor (6.9\\%), so temperature-induced variance would predominantly affect the ambiguous condition, preserving or widening the gap. A temperature $= 0.7$ pilot across three model families (GPT-4.1, Claude Sonnet~4.6, DeepSeek-V3; Appendix~\\ref{app:temperature}) confirms that the treatment effect is robust (85--97\\% clause-level agreement with greedy decoding), though the remaining two models and extreme-temperature regimes remain untested; the per-model pilot ($n = 50$ pairs) is powered to detect effects $\\geq$24.6\\,pp at 80\\% power, so smaller attenuations at higher temperatures cannot be ruled out. Three additional scope limitations warrant disclosure: (1) three of the five judges share the OpenAI model family; however, GPT-5.4-judged and GPT-4.1-judged models show similar mean violation rates (38.0\\% vs.\\ 45.3\\%, driven by DeepSeek's high base rate rather than judge leniency; cross-model judge $\\kappa = 0.653$, McNemar $p = 0.606$), suggesting the 3:2 allocation does not systematically inflate convergence estimates; (2) authorization scope has the lowest per-type judge agreement ($\\kappa = 0.385$, $n \\approx 17$), so conclusions about this type carry wider uncertainty; (3) the coreferential type, whose variants differ only in referent specification, was excluded from both the mitigation and SOPBench replication analyses, limiting generalization for that type; and (4) the ICC analysis uses only within-family variation (GPT-4o/4o-mini), so the reported reliability bounds may not hold across architecturally diverse model pairs. Future work should extend to non-customer-service domains and collect per-episode human mechanism annotations to validate the type--mechanism association.",
          "+Our ambiguity pairs are synthetically constructed, so the 34.0\\,pp effect is an upper bound; the SOPBench evaluation ($+$12.7\\,pp unweighted mean) provides a cross-benchmark lower bound. Failure mode coding relies on LLM annotators ($\\kappa = 0.52$; human $\\kappa = 0.32$, rising to $0.41$ after collapsing the spurious-compliance/structural-misparse boundary; improved guidelines, prior round $0.15$), though the coder prompt excludes ambiguity type labels, preventing type--mechanism contamination. All experiments use temperature $= 0$, measuring each model's modal interpretation---the policy-relevant quantity agents will most likely commit to and the typical deployment setting for policy-governed agents. The matched-pair design partially mitigates temperature effects: any variance increase at higher temperatures applies to both conditions, so the treatment effect direction is unlikely to reverse. Moreover, the unambiguous-condition violation rate is already near floor (6.9\\%), so temperature-induced variance would predominantly affect the ambiguous condition, preserving or widening the gap. A temperature $= 0.7$ pilot across three model families (GPT-4.1, Claude Sonnet~4.6, DeepSeek-V3; Appendix~\\ref{app:temperature}) confirms that the treatment effect is robust (85--97\\% clause-level agreement with greedy decoding), though the remaining two models and extreme-temperature regimes remain untested; the per-model pilot ($n = 50$ pairs) is powered to detect effects $\\geq$24.6\\,pp at 80\\% power, so smaller attenuations at higher temperatures cannot be ruled out. Three additional scope limitations warrant disclosure: (1) three of the five judges share the OpenAI model family; however, GPT-5.4-judged and GPT-4.1-judged models show similar mean violation rates (38.0\\% vs.\\ 45.3\\%, driven by DeepSeek's high base rate rather than judge leniency; cross-model judge $\\kappa = 0.653$, McNemar $p = 0.606$), suggesting the 3:2 allocation does not systematically inflate convergence estimates; (2) authorization scope has the lowest per-type judge agreement ($\\kappa = 0.385$, $n \\approx 17$), so conclusions about this type carry wider uncertainty; (3) the coreferential type, whose variants differ only in referent specification, was excluded from both the mitigation and SOPBench replication analyses, limiting generalization for that type; and (4) the ICC analysis uses only within-family variation (GPT-4o/4o-mini), so the reported reliability bounds may not hold across architecturally diverse model pairs. All evaluated models are frontier-class ($\\geq$100B parameters or equivalent capability tier); generalization to smaller open-source models (7B--70B) remains untested.   "old_string": "Our taxonomy uses \\emph{ambiguity} as a policy specification umbrella term encompassing two epistemologically distinct operations. Five types involve \\emph{interpretation selection}: the text admits multiple readings and the agent commits to one. Incompleteness involves \\emph{information addition}: the text lacks necessary content entirely, and the agent confabulates it. This distinction manifests empirically: incompleteness is the only type unresponsive to prompt-based disambiguation ($-$3.4\\,pp, n.s.; \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}) because prompts cannot supply absent domain knowledge. We retain both under a single umbrella because agents process all six types identically---committing without flagging uncertainty---and the resulting violations produce comparable safety impact (\\S\\ref{sec:results}). This inclusive scope aligns with RE usage, where ambiguity and incompleteness frequently co-occur \\citep{dalpiaz_pinpointing_2018}.",
  "new_string": "Our taxonomy uses \\emph{ambiguity} as a policy specification umbrella term encompassing two epistemologically distinct operations. We acknowledge that incompleteness occupies a contested boundary between ambiguity and underspecification in the RE literature; however, \\citet{berry_kamsties_2004}'s nocuous ambiguity taxonomy itself includes specification-level gaps, and \\citet{gleich_ambiguity_2010} treat missing information as ``implicit ambiguity.'' Five types involve \\emph{interpretation selection}: the text admits multiple readings and the agent commits to one. Incompleteness involves \\emph{information addition}: the text lacks necessary content entirely, and the agent confabulates it. This distinction manifests empirically: incompleteness is the only type unresponsive to prompt-based disambiguation ($-$3.4\\,pp, n.s.; \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}) because prompts cannot supply absent domain knowledge. We retain both under a single umbrella because agents process all six types identically---committing without flagging uncertainty---and the resulting violations produce comparable safety impact (\\S\\ref{sec:results}). This inclusive scope aligns with RE usage, where ambiguity and incompleteness frequently co-occur \\citep{dalpiaz_pinpointing_2018}."
}

This inclusive scope aligns with RE usage, where ambiguity and incompleteness frequently co-occur \\citep{dalpiaz_pinpointing_2018}.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Ambiguity Taxonomy}\n\\label{sec:taxonomy}\n\nWe adopt an inclusive definition of ambiguity that encompasses phenomena sometimes distinguished as underspecification or vagueness \\citep{poesio_artstein_2005,vandeemter_semantic_1996}: a policy clause is ambiguous when it admits multiple distinct interpretations available to a competent reader, including cases where the boundary between interpretations is gradient rather than discrete \\citep{wasow_ambiguity_2005,kennedy_vagueness_2007}. In the policy specification domain, these distinctions are less salient than the shared property of admitting multiple operationally distinct agent behaviors.\n\nTraditional NLP ambiguity classification focuses on within-sentence phenomena \\citep{wasow_ambiguity_2005}; our specification layer captures document-level policy gaps unstudied in the agent context. Our taxonomy provides an organizational framework of six types organized into two layers (Table~\\ref{tab:taxonomy}), selected from \\citet{li_ambiguity_taxonomy_2024}'s NLP taxonomy (11 types; full mapping in Appendix~\\ref{app:li_mapping}) based on three design criteria: (1)~theoretical attestation in prior RE literature \\citep{massey_ambiguity_re_2014,berry_kamsties_2004,drip_r_2026}, (2)~amenability to minimal-pair construction without altering clause semantics beyond the target ambiguity, and (3)~mutual exclusivity for unambiguous IV assignment. Types such as ellipsis and pragmatic implicature were excluded because they resist minimal-pair construction within the single-clause policy format.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\footnotesize\n\\caption{Six-type ambiguity taxonomy. Disambiguation channel indicates the primary mechanism required to resolve the ambiguity.}\n\\label{tab:taxonomy}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llll@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Type} & \\textbf{Layer} & \\textbf{Definition} & \\textbf{Disambig.\\ Channel} \\\\\n\\midrule\nLexical & Ling. & Word/phrase admits multiple senses & Context \\\\\nScopal & Ling. & Quantifier/operator scope underdetermined & Bracketing \\\\\nCoreferential & Ling. & Pronoun/NP referent is ambiguous & Resolution \\\\\nAuth.\\ Scope & Spec. & Permission boundary is vague & Boundary def. \\\\\nIncompleteness & Spec. & Required information is absent & Content addition \\\\\nCond.\\ Prec. & Spec. & Rules conflict under certain conditions & Priority rule \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}%\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Linguistic layer.} These types operate at the syntactic or semantic level and can, in principle, be resolved by contextual cues within the text.\n\\emph{Scopal} ambiguity involves quantifier, negation, or modal scope interactions (e.g., ``all items may not be returned'' admits universal-negation and negation-of-universal readings).\n\\emph{Lexical} ambiguity covers polysemy or homonymy (e.g., ``charge'' as financial cost vs.\\ penalty).\n\\emph{Coreferential} ambiguity involves unclear pronoun or NP reference (e.g., ``\\ldots which must be approved by a supervisor'' where ``which'' could refer to the verification or the refund).\n\n\\paragraph{Specification layer.} These types arise from pragmatic or discourse-level gaps requiring domain knowledge to resolve.\n\\emph{Authorization scope} covers vague permission or role boundaries (e.g., ``authorized personnel may issue refunds'' without specifying which personnel).\n\\emph{Incompleteness} denotes missing conditions or boundary cases (e.g., a return policy silent on damaged items); we control for its information-addition confound via logistic regression (\\S\\ref{sec:results}).\n\\emph{Conditional precedence} involves conflicting rules without priority ordering (e.g., ``premium customers receive free shipping'' vs.\\ ``orders under \\$50 require a shipping fee'').\n\nThe layer distinction reflects a design hypothesis motivated by prior typologies \\citep{massey_ambiguity_re_2014,berry_kamsties_2004}: linguistic ambiguity can in principle be partially resolved through tool responses or world knowledge, while specification ambiguity has no external resolution channel. This hypothesis motivates systematic coverage across both sources; its empirical status is reported in \\S\\ref{sec:results} (C3, inconclusive).\n\n\\paragraph{Epistemological status.}\nOur taxonomy uses \\emph{ambiguity} as a policy specification umbrella term encompassing two epistemologically distinct operations. Five types involve \\emph{interpretation selection}: the text admits multiple readings and the agent commits to one. Incompleteness involves \\emph{information addition}: the text lacks necessary content entirely, and the agent confabulates it. This distinction manifests empirically: incompleteness is the only type unresponsive to prompt-based disambiguation ($-$3.4\\,pp, n.s.; \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}) because prompts cannot supply absent domain knowledge. We retain both under a single umbrella because agents process all six types identically---committing without flagging uncertainty---and the resulting violations produce comparable safety impact (\\S\\ref{sec:results}). This inclusive scope aligns with RE usage, where ambiguity and incompleteness frequently co-occur \\citep{dalpiaz_pinpointing_2018}.\n",

  "new_string": "Bootstrap clustering ($B = 10{,}000$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cluster}) confirms stable extremes across all margins. Per-type inter-judge agreement ranges from $\\kappa = 0.875$ (incompleteness) to $\\kappa = 0.385$ (authorization scope), with intermediate values for conditional precedence ($0.762$), lexical ($0.712$), scopal ($0.538$), and coreferential ($0.455$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_judge}).     "originalFile": "\\section{Results}\n\\label{sec:results}\n\nWe organize results around three analyses: the binary ambiguity effect (C1), the type partial ordering (C2, including the inconclusive layer effect C3), and the model$\\times$type interaction (C4). C1 and C2 are primary supported claims; C3 (layer effect) is inconclusive; C4 (model$\\times$type interaction) is exploratory.\n\n\\subsection{Binary Ambiguity Effect (C1)}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_variance_decomposition.pdf}\n\\caption{Variance decomposition of violation rates (temperature $= 0$). Clause identity: 22.4\\% (0.4\\% between types); condition: 15.8\\%; model: 1.0\\%; residual: 60.8\\%. Clause wording---not type membership---is the primary risk factor among measured predictors.}\n\\label{fig:variance_decomposition}\n\\end{figure}\n\nAcross all five models and six types, ambiguous clauses increase violation rates by 34.0 pp (41.0\\% vs.\\ 6.9\\%, McNemar OR $= 15.1$ (based on $n = 580$ discordant pairs), 95\\% CI $[10.78, 21.17]$, $p < 10^{-105}$). All six types individually produce significant increases (all $p < 10^{-5}$) across both retail ($+$30.6\\,pp) and airline ($+$37.7\\,pp) domains (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_domain}), and all five models show large effects ($+25.7$ to $+39.0$ pp, all $p < 10^{-17}$). Perturbation controls confirm specificity to semantic ambiguity (\\S\\ref{sec:method}). Figure~\\ref{fig:variance_decomposition} decomposes violation-rate variance\\footnote{Type III SS; logistic regression (Appendix~\\ref{app:regression}) confirms concordant results (Tjur's $R^2 = 0.175$; \\citealp{tjur_2009}).}: clause identity explains 22.4\\% (0.4\\% between types, 22.0\\% within), condition 15.8\\%, model 1.0\\%, and residual 60.8\\% ($\\sim$15\\% stochastic, $\\sim$46\\% systematic; Appendix~\\ref{app:residual}). Excluding incompleteness ($+$49.2\\,pp, subject to information-addition confound; Appendix~\\ref{app:confound}), the effect is $+$31.0\\,pp (95\\% CI $[27.9, 34.1]$; Appendix~\\ref{app:sans_incompleteness}). The confound-minimized coreferential type ($\\Delta$token $\\approx 7.8$ words) still produces $+$18.7\\,pp, isolating the ambiguity-only contribution. $\\Delta_{\\text{token}}$ is a coarse proxy for information content; authorization scope exhibits similar token differences ($\\Delta = 36.1$ words) without the information-addition confound.\n\nConvergent evidence from two additional models confirms the effect across seven models ($\\Delta = +20.0$ to $+33.6$\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:model_expansion}); cross-benchmark evaluation on SOPBench~\\citep{li_sopbench_2025} shows directional consistency ($+12.7$\\,pp unweighted mean; Appendix~\\ref{app:sopbench}). The taxonomy predicts failure mechanisms more strongly than violation rate (NMI $= 0.23$ vs.\\ $0.01$; $V = 0.45$ vs.\\ $0.14$ on tables of different dimensionality; Appendix~\\ref{app:type_mechanism}), making type-level analysis (C2--C4) informative for diagnosis.\n\n\\FloatBarrier\n\\subsection{Type Partial Ordering (C2)}\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Violation rates (\\%) under ambiguous policy clauses by type and model (5.4 = GPT-5.4, 4.1 = GPT-4.1, Cla.\\ = Claude, Qw3 = Qwen3, DS = DeepSeek). $\\Delta$pp: pooled difference (ambiguous $-$ unambiguous) with Newcombe 95\\% CIs. Cluster: TOST equivalence cluster at $\\pm 15$\\,pp---U = upper bound (separated at all margins), M = mid-tier (6/6 pairwise equivalent), L = lower (separates at $\\pm 12$\\,pp). Layer grouping (Spec./Ling.) did not survive correction (C3, inconclusive; \\S\\ref{sec:results}). All per-type $p < 10^{-5}$ (Fisher's exact).}\n\\label{tab:main_results}\n\\footnotesize\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3.8pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{llrrrrrrr@{\\hskip 6pt}cr}\n\\toprule\nLayer & Type & Pooled & 5.4 & 4.1 & Cla. & Qw3 & DS & $\\Delta$pp\\,[95\\% CI] & Cl. & $p$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{3}{*}{\\textcolor{speccolor}{Spec.}} & Incompleteness$^\\dagger$ & 53.6 & 46 & 58 & 48 & 62 & 54 & +49.2\\,[42.2, 55.6] & U & $<\\!10^{-37}$ \\\\\n & Auth.\\ Scope$^\\ddagger$ & 41.6 & 64 & 46 & 12 & 26 & 60 & +36.0\\,[29.1, 42.6] & M & $<\\!10^{-22}$ \\\\\n & Cond.\\ Prec. & 36.8 & 34 & 36 & 36 & 40 & 38 & +30.8\\,[24.0, 37.4] & M & $<\\!10^{-17}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{3}{*}{\\textcolor{lingcolor}{Ling.}} & Lexical & 42.8 & 42 & 34 & 32 & 52 & 54 & +35.2\\,[28.0, 42.0] & M & $<\\!10^{-20}$ \\\\\n & Scopal & 39.4 & 42 & 38 & 32 & 37 & 48 & +34.2\\,[27.4, 40.7] & M & $<\\!10^{-21}$ \\\\\n & Coreferential & 31.6 & 18 & 32 & 22 & 42 & 44 & +18.7\\,[11.5, 25.7] & L & $<\\!10^{-6}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{2}{l}{\\textbf{Overall}} & \\textbf{41.0} & 41 & 41 & 30 & 43 & 50 & \\textbf{+34.0}\\,[31.3, 36.9] & --- & $<\\!10^{-105}$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\n\\vspace{2pt}\n{\\footnotesize $\\dagger$\\,Subject to information-addition confound; see \\S\\ref{sec:results} and Appendix~\\ref{app:confound}. $\\ddagger$\\,Lowest inter-judge agreement ($\\kappa = 0.385$, per-type calibration $n \\approx 17$); $\\Delta$token$\\,= 36.1$ words, subject to information-addition confound analogous to incompleteness; see \\S\\ref{sec:results}. Excluding incompleteness: pooled $\\Delta = +31.0$\\,pp (95\\% CI $[27.9, 34.1]$).}\n\\end{table*}\n\nThe six types are not interchangeable. An omnibus $\\chi^2$ test on ambiguous-condition violation rates rejects the uniformity null ($\\chi^2 = 28.02$, $df = 5$, $p = 3.6 \\times 10^{-5}$, Cram\\'{e}r's $V = 0.137$). Table~\\ref{tab:tost_sensitivity} reveals a margin-dependent equivalence structure: three types achieve equivalence at $\\pm 10$\\,pp, five at $\\pm 12$\\,pp, and all six at $\\pm 15$\\,pp; we present the full sensitivity analysis without anchoring to a single margin. The resulting partial ordering is exploratory and contingent on the $\\pm 15$\\,pp SESOI choice (Table~\\ref{tab:tost_sensitivity} sensitivity columns show instability below $\\pm 12$\\,pp). The NMI comparison ($0.23$ vs.\\ $0.01$) is threshold-independent. Table~\\ref{tab:main_results} and Figure~\\ref{fig:forest_plot} present the full breakdown.\n\nBootstrap clustering ($B = 10{,}000$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cluster}) confirms stable extremes across all margins. Cross-model concordance is moderate ($W = 0.398$, $p = 0.072$; Appendix~\\ref{app:kendall_w}). Authorization scope exhibits the widest cross-model range (12\\%--64\\%) and the lowest per-type judge agreement ($\\kappa = 0.385$); conclusions about this type carry wider uncertainty than the pooled estimates.\n\nCross-model judge validation ($\\rho = 0.71$--$0.89$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_judge}) confirms type-ranking stability when model composition is held constant; the low two-group concordance ($\\rho = 0.09$; Appendix~\\ref{app:judge_group_ranking}) reflects differential vulnerability to authorization scope$^\\ddagger$ (12\\%--64\\% range), not ranking instability. The partial ordering has robust extremes---incompleteness as upper bound and coreferential as lower bound, replicated across seven models and three judges---with model-composition-dependent mid-tier structure.\n\nIncompleteness ranks highest in four of five models ($\\geq$46\\% in all seven; Appendix~\\ref{app:model_expansion}); its 49.2\\,pp effect is an upper bound (Appendix~\\ref{app:confound}). C3 (layer grouping) did not survive Holm--Bonferroni ($p = 0.017$, threshold $0.0125$).\n\n\\begin{figure}[!ht]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_forest_plot.pdf}\n\\caption{Per-type effect sizes ($\\Delta$pp) with Newcombe 95\\% CIs (overall: $+$34.0\\,pp, OR $= 15.1$). Types form a partial ordering: incompleteness as upper bound (conditional on information-addition confound), coreferential as lower bound, and a mid-range equivalence cluster. Mid-tier ordering is margin-dependent (\\S\\ref{sec:results}).}\n\\label{fig:forest_plot}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\label{sec:c4}C4 (model$\\times$type interaction) is exploratory and driven solely by authorization scope; details in Appendix~\\ref{app:c4_sans_auth}.\n\n",

963	\paragraph{Power analysis.} With $k = 5$ runs per clause pair (GPT-4.1), the within-model SD of the treatment effect is 3.3\,pp, yielding SE $= 1.5$\,pp. A paired comparison of $\Delta_{t=0}$ vs.\ $\Delta_{t=0.7}$ could detect a temperature-induced shift of $\geq$4.7\,pp at 80\% power ($\alpha = 0.05$). The observed shift ($-$0.4\,pp) is well below this threshold. For $k = 3$ models (Claude, DeepSeek), power is lower: detectable shift $\geq$8--10\,pp. All observed shifts are $< 5$\,pp, but we cannot rule out moderate temperature effects (5--10\,pp) for these models.
964	
965	\section{Use of AI Assistants}
966	\label{app:ai-assistants}
967

222	
223	Descriptively, authorization scope has the lowest human--LLM $\kappa$ among the four substantively high-rate types (0.644), consistent with its lowest cross-judge $\kappa$ (0.385, \S\ref{sec:method}). Both reflect the inherent difficulty of permission-boundary judgments rather than systematic judge failure: raw agreement is still 85.0\%, and the 34\,pp within-judge-group range (\S\ref{sec:c4}) far exceeds measurement noise. Coreferential's low $\kappa$ (0.479) reflects the prevalence paradox---its violation base rate is 8.8\%, compressing the $\kappa$ denominator despite 85.0\% raw agreement. Per-type estimates are imprecise due to small cell sizes ($n = 32$--$34$); they are descriptive statistics only and should not be used for type-level reliability ranking or inference.
224	
225	\section{Information Delta Logistic Regression}
226	\label{app:regression}
227	
228	Table~\ref{tab:logistic_regression} reports the full logistic regression controlling for token-count difference between ambiguous and unambiguous variants (information delta). The reference category is authorization scope (type) and Claude Sonnet 4.6 (model). Token delta is significant ($p < 0.001$) but small in magnitude (OR $= 1.022$ per token), indicating that information quantity partially confounds the type effect. Critically, incompleteness remains individually significant ($p = 0.018$) after controlling for token delta, with a coefficient change of $<$8\% relative to the model without the covariate. The slightly lower $p$-value compared to the univariate confound control in Appendix~\ref{app:confound} ($p = 0.022$) reflects the additional variance absorbed by model covariates in the full specification.
229

```text
256	\end{table}
257	
258	\section{Bootstrap Cluster Analysis}
259	\label{app:cluster}
260	
261	To complement the margin-dependent TOST analysis, we apply Ward's hierarchical clustering to the six observed type-level violation rates, bootstrapped $B = 10{,}000$ times by resampling clause pairs within each type. At $k = 2$, incompleteness (53.6\%) forms a singleton cluster, with the remaining five types (31.6--42.8\%) in the second cluster, reproducing in 34.3\% of bootstrap samples---the most frequent partition but not overwhelmingly stable, reflecting that the 10.8\,pp gap between incompleteness and lexical is moderate relative to within-type variance. At $k = 3$, coreferential (31.6\%) additionally separates, consistent with the TOST equivalence structure. The AU $p$-value for the incompleteness singleton is 0.47, confirming that while the singleton is the modal partition, the boundary is not sharp.
262	
263	\section{Clause-Level Variance}
264	\label{app:clause_variance}
265	
266	Leave-one-out analysis confirms that no single clause drives any type-level rate: maximum LOO shift is 1.4\,pp (coreferential), and all types have LOO ranges $\leq$2.1\,pp. Within-type coefficients of variation (CV) range from 0.69 (incompleteness) to 1.03 (coreferential), confirming high clause-level heterogeneity. This heterogeneity is expected---clause wording determines the specific ambiguity instance---and is controlled by the matched-pair design, which compares each clause's ambiguous and unambiguous variants rather than relying on type-level means.
267	
268	A stratified permutation test (1,000 iterations, shuffling clause-level violation rates within type$\times$condition$\times$model strata) yields a null $R^2$ distribution with mean 8.3\% (SD $= 0.6\%$, 95th percentile $= 9.4\%$, max $= 10.2\%$), confirming that the observed 22.4\% far exceeds chance expectation ($p < 0.001$; 0 of 1,000 permutations exceeded the observed value). The confound-adjusted clause effect (observed minus null mean) is 14.1 percentage points.
269	
270	Per-clause $\Delta$ (averaged across five models) has mean 34.0\,pp, median 20.0\,pp (right-skewed, skewness $= 0.33$): 197 clauses show positive effects, 89 show zero, and 14 show negative effects. The wide IQR (0--60\,pp) confirms high within-type heterogeneity consistent with the low type-level variance (0.4\%).
271	
272	\paragraph{Concordant pair decomposition.} Of 1{,}497 matched pairs (300 clauses $\times$ 5 models, minus 3 API-error episodes), 580 are discordant (38.7\%): 544 (93.8\%) show ambiguous-only violations and 36 (6.2\%) unambiguous-only. Of the 917 concordant pairs, 849 (56.7\%) are concordant non-violations and 68 (4.5\%) are concordant violations---the latter representing clauses that are problematic even without ambiguity.
273	
274	\section{Multiple Comparison Correction}
275	\label{app:multiple_comparison}
```

  "new_string": "\\section{Mantel-Haenszel Stratified Analysis}\n\\label{app:mantel_haenszel}\n\nTo verify robustness of the pooled OR to model heterogeneity, we compute the Mantel-Haenszel common OR stratified by model. All five per-model ORs are significant ($p < 10^{-15}$): Claude $= 8.89$, DeepSeek $= 8.26$, GPT-4.1 $= 10.74$, GPT-5.4 $= 25.36$, Qwen3 $= 6.31$. The Breslow-Day test rejects homogeneity ($\\chi^2 = 11.45$, $df = 4$, $p = 0.022$), driven by GPT-5.4's elevated OR; however, all five models show large, individually significant effects in the same direction.\n\n\\section{Bootstrap Cluster Analysis}\n\\label{app:cluster}"
}

This session is being continued from a previous conversation that ran out of context. The summary below covers the earlier portion of the conversation.

Summary:
1. Primary Request and Intent:
   The Director issued **R33 review fix instructions** — a comprehensive 10-task assignment with <48h deadline for an EMNLP 2026 ARR paper on policy ambiguity safety in LLM agents. Tasks span three phases:
   - **Phase 1 (Computation)**: SF4 ambient baseline verification, MF1-B logistic regression with token interaction, MF2-A misclassification sensitivity analysis, SF5 Mantel-Haenszel stratified OR
   - **Phase 2 (Writing)**: MF1-A epistemological expansion in taxonomy, MF2-B per-type κ presentation, SF1 partial ordering exploratory label, SF2 SOPBench wording changes, SF3 model tier in limitations, Visual p.28 layout fix
   - **Phase 3 (Verification)**: Compile check, page budget, report all results to Director

   Prior to R33, the session completed Figure 1 redesign (round 5) using matplotlib (PaperBanana failed with Gemini API errors).

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - Matched-pair causal design: 300 clause pairs × 5 models × 2 conditions = 2,997 episodes
   - Six-type ambiguity taxonomy: authorization_scope, incompleteness, conditional_precedence, lexical, scopal, coreferential
   - Convergent nocuity: 41.3% ambiguous clauses trigger ≥3/5 model convergent violations; ambient baseline 1.7%
   - Information-addition confound: incompleteness adds information (high Δtoken), not just disambiguates; logistic regression interaction p=0.0004 but effect at Δtoken=0 still +20.5pp
   - Misclassification sensitivity: random measurement error attenuates matched-pair effects toward zero; coreferential +18.1pp robust at 25% error rate (p=0.011)
   - Mantel-Haenszel OR: 9.57 [7.63, 12.01] stratified by model; Breslow-Day p=0.022 (heterogeneous, driven by GPT-5.4 OR=25.36)
   - Per-type inter-judge κ (dual-judge calibration, n=100): incomp 0.875, cond_prec 0.762, lexical 0.712, scopal 0.538, coref 0.455, auth_scope 0.385
   - Font scaling for print figures: 16" source → 6.7" textwidth display = 0.42× scale factor; minimum 14pt source → ~6pt print

3. Files and Code Sections:

   - **`docs/paper/figures/fig1_overview.py`** (rewritten, ~330 lines)
     - Three-panel matplotlib figure for EMNLP paper
     - Font size tiers: FS = {min:14, body:16, label:18, subhead:20, title:24, panel:26, hero:48, delta:28}
     - Panel widths: ax_a=[0.01,0.02,0.27,0.96], ax_b=[0.30,0.02,0.35,0.96], ax_c=[0.67,0.02,0.32,0.96]
     - Coordinate system 0-10 per panel, figsize=(16, 5.2), 300 DPI
     - Panel (a): Taxonomy boxes (spec blue #3498DB, ling green #27AE60) + matched clause pair example
     - Panel (b): Policy Doc → fork (ambiguous/unambiguous) → τ²-bench → Trace, Δ=Causal Effect annotation, 5 model icons
     - Panel (c): Evaluation pipeline (Episode→LLM Judge→4-tier severity), Key Findings (+34.0pp hero number, convergent nocuity 5-circle→VIOLATION visualization)
     - Panel dividers added as fig.lines with dashed gray (#E0E0E0)

   - **`docs/paper/sections/analysis.tex`** — Added Task 1 ambient baseline sentence:
     - Added before nocuity sans incompleteness: `"Under unambiguous conditions, the ambient convergent violation rate is 1.7\% (5/300 clauses with $\geq$3/5 model violations), confirming that the observed 41.3\% under ambiguity reflects a $+$39.6\,pp ambiguity-induced excess rather than ambient model correlation."`

   - **`docs/paper/sections/experiments.tex`** — Multiple edits:
     - Task 7 (SF1): Added after TOST sentence: `"The resulting partial ordering is exploratory and contingent on the $\pm 15$\,pp SESOI choice (Table~\ref{tab:tost_sensitivity} sensitivity columns show instability below $\pm 12$\,pp)."`
     - Task 8 (SF2): Changed "Replication on two additional models" → "Convergent evidence from two additional models"; "SOPBench confirms" → "cross-benchmark evaluation on SOPBench shows"
     - Task 6 (MF2-B): Added per-type κ paragraph after cross-model concordance sentence: reports all 6 per-type κ values from dual_judge_calibration, notes coreferential and scopal CIs include zero, adds attenuation argument

   - **`docs/paper/sections/taxonomy.tex`** — Task 5 (MF1-A):
     - Expanded epistemological status paragraph. Added 2 sentences before the interpretation selection/information addition distinction: `"We acknowledge that incompleteness occupies a contested boundary between ambiguity and underspecification in the RE literature; however, \citet{berry_kamsties_2004}'s nocuous ambiguity taxonomy itself includes specification-level gaps, and \citet{gleich_ambiguity_2010} treat missing information as ``implicit ambiguity.''"`

   - **`docs/paper/sections/discussion.tex`** — Task 8 (SF2):
     - Replaced SOPBench mention with: `"Three structural differences between benchmarks contribute to the magnitude gap (Appendix~\ref{app:sopbench}); the $+$12.7\,pp SOPBench estimate provides a naturalistic lower bound."`

   - **`docs/paper/sections/abstract.tex`** — Task 8 (SF2):
     - Changed "cross-benchmark replication on SOPBench confirms" → "cross-benchmark evaluation on SOPBench shows"

   - **`docs/paper/sections/limitations.tex`** — Tasks 8 + 9:
     - Task 8: "SOPBench replication" → "SOPBench evaluation"
     - Task 9: Added before final sentence: `"All evaluated models are frontier-class ($\geq$100B parameters or equivalent capability tier); generalization to smaller open-source models (7B--70B) remains untested."`

   - **`docs/paper/sections/introduction.tex`** — Tasks 8 + Figure 1 caption:
     - Task 8: "replicated on SOPBench" → "with convergent evidence on SOPBench"
     - Figure 1 caption updated to three-panel description: "(a) Six-type ambiguity taxonomy... (b) Matched-pair causal design... (c) Cross-model judge evaluation..."

   - **`docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex`** — Multiple edits:
     - Task 8: Section title "Cross-Benchmark Replication (SOPBench)" → "Cross-Benchmark Evaluation (SOPBench)"; "we replicate" → "we evaluate"; table caption "replication" → "evaluation"; "key replication signal" → "key signal"
     - Task 4 writing: Added new section `\section{Mantel-Haenszel Stratified Analysis} \label{app:mantel_haenszel}` with MH OR=9.57, per-model ORs, Breslow-Day test results
     - Task 10: Compacted "Use of AI Assistants" from bullet list to single paragraph

   - **`docs/paper/references.bib`** — Added Gleich et al. 2010 entry:

       year={2010},

   - **Computation artifacts created by background agents:**
     - `artifacts/analysis_ambient_baseline.json` — Task 1: 5/300 unambiguous convergent, 124/300 ambiguous convergent
     - `artifacts/analysis_logistic_token_interaction.json` — Task 2: condition×Δtoken interaction coef=0.033, p=0.0004; effect at Δtoken=0 = +20.5pp
     - `artifacts/analysis_misclassification_sensitivity.json` — Task 3: coreferential at 25% error = +9.2pp (p=0.011); all-types at 30% = +13.5pp (p<0.0001)
     - `artifacts/analysis_mantel_haenszel.json` — Task 4: MH OR=9.57 [7.63, 12.01]; Breslow-Day p=0.022

   - **Data files used:**
     - `artifacts/full_study/analysis/per_clause_summary.csv` (2997 rows) — clause_id, ambiguity_type, condition, model, violation_rate, n_episodes, n_violations, modal_level
     - `artifacts/clause_templates.json` (90 entries) — includes ambiguous_clause, unambiguous_clause text
     - `artifacts/full_study/analysis/dual_judge_calibration.json` — per-type inter-judge κ (n=100)
     - `artifacts/round6_supplementary/per_type_human_agreement.json` — per-type human-LLM κ (n=200)

   - **Google API key polling failure**: `poll_secret_request('secret_req_google_api_key_1779628878', 'policy_ambiguity_safety')` returned "Request not found". Continued with matplotlib approach rather than retrying PaperBanana.

  "attachment": {

      "type": "text",

        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-be-idea-gen-0519-4/projects/policy_ambiguity_safety/docs/paper/sections/experiments.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Results}\n\\label{sec:results}\n\nWe organize results around three analyses: the binary ambiguity effect (C1), the type partial ordering (C2, including the inconclusive layer effect C3), and the model$\\times$type interaction (C4). C1 and C2 are primary supported claims; C3 (layer effect) is inconclusive; C4 (model$\\times$type interaction) is exploratory.\n\n\\subsection{Binary Ambiguity Effect (C1)}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_variance_decomposition.pdf}\n\\caption{Variance decomposition of violation rates (temperature $= 0$). Clause identity: 22.4\\% (0.4\\% between types); condition: 15.8\\%; model: 1.0\\%; residual: 60.8\\%. Clause wording---not type membership---is the primary risk factor among measured predictors.}\n\\label{fig:variance_decomposition}\n\\end{figure}\n\nAcross all five models and six types, ambiguous clauses increase violation rates by 34.0 pp (41.0\\% vs.\\ 6.9\\%, McNemar OR $= 15.1$ (based on $n = 580$ discordant pairs), 95\\% CI $[10.78, 21.17]$, $p < 10^{-105}$). All six types individually produce significant increases (all $p < 10^{-5}$) across both retail ($+$30.6\\,pp) and airline ($+$37.7\\,pp) domains (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_domain}), and all five models show large effects ($+25.7$ to $+39.0$ pp, all $p < 10^{-17}$). Perturbation controls confirm specificity to semantic ambiguity (\\S\\ref{sec:method}). Figure~\\ref{fig:variance_decomposition} decomposes violation-rate variance\\footnote{Type III SS; logistic regression (Appendix~\\ref{app:regression}) confirms concordant results (Tjur's $R^2 = 0.175$; \\citealp{tjur_2009}).}: clause identity explains 22.4\\% (0.4\\% between types, 22.0\\% within), condition 15.8\\%, model 1.0\\%, and residual 60.8\\% ($\\sim$15\\% stochastic, $\\sim$46\\% systematic; Appendix~\\ref{app:residual}). Excluding incompleteness ($+$49.2\\,pp, subject to information-addition confound; Appendix~\\ref{app:confound}), the effect is $+$31.0\\,pp (95\\% CI $[27.9, 34.1]$; Appendix~\\ref{app:sans_incompleteness}). The confound-minimized coreferential type ($\\Delta$token $\\approx 7.8$ words) still produces $+$18.7\\,pp, isolating the ambiguity-only contribution. $\\Delta_{\\text{token}}$ is a coarse proxy for information content; authorization scope exhibits similar token differences ($\\Delta = 36.1$ words) without the information-addition confound.\n\nConvergent evidence from two additional models confirms the effect across seven models ($\\Delta = +20.0$ to $+33.6$\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:model_expansion}); cross-benchmark evaluation on SOPBench~\\citep{li_sopbench_2025} shows directional consistency ($+12.7$\\,pp unweighted mean; Appendix~\\ref{app:sopbench}). The taxonomy predicts failure mechanisms more strongly than violation rate (NMI $= 0.23$ vs.\\ $0.01$; $V = 0.45$ vs.\\ $0.14$ on tables of different dimensionality; Appendix~\\ref{app:type_mechanism}), making type-level analysis (C2--C4) informative for diagnosis.\n\n\\FloatBarrier\n\\subsection{Type Partial Ordering (C2)}\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Violation rates (\\%) under ambiguous policy clauses by type and model (5.4 = GPT-5.4, 4.1 = GPT-4.1, Cla.\\ = Claude, Qw3 = Qwen3, DS = DeepSeek). $\\Delta$pp: pooled difference (ambiguous $-$ unambiguous) with Newcombe 95\\% CIs. Cluster: TOST equivalence cluster at $\\pm 15$\\,pp---U = upper bound (separated at all margins), M = mid-tier (6/6 pairwise equivalent), L = lower (separates at $\\pm 12$\\,pp). Layer grouping (Spec./Ling.) did not survive correction (C3, inconclusive; \\S\\ref{sec:results}). All per-type $p < 10^{-5}$ (Fisher's exact).}\n\\label{tab:main_results}\n\\footnotesize\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3.8pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{llrrrrrrr@{\\hskip 6pt}cr}\n\\toprule\nLayer & Type & Pooled & 5.4 & 4.1 & Cla. & Qw3 & DS & $\\Delta$pp\\,[95\\% CI] & Cl. & $p$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{3}{*}{\\textcolor{speccolor}{Spec.}} & Incompleteness$^\\dagger$ & 53.6 & 46 & 58 & 48 & 62 & 54 & +49.2\\,[42.2, 55.6] & U & $<\\!10^{-37}$ \\\\\n & Auth.\\ Scope$^\\ddagger$ & 41.6 & 64 & 46 & 12 & 26 & 60 & +36.0\\,[29.1, 42.6] & M & $<\\!10^{-22}$ \\\\\n & Cond.\\ Prec. & 36.8 & 34 & 36 & 36 & 40 & 38 & +30.8\\,[24.0, 37.4] & M & $<\\!10^{-17}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{3}{*}{\\textcolor{lingcolor}{Ling.}} & Lexical & 42.8 & 42 & 34 & 32 & 52 & 54 & +35.2\\,[28.0, 42.0] & M & $<\\!10^{-20}$ \\\\\n & Scopal & 39.4 & 42 & 38 & 32 & 37 & 48 & +34.2\\,[27.4, 40.7] & M & $<\\!10^{-21}$ \\\\\n & Coreferential & 31.6 & 18 & 32 & 22 & 42 & 44 & +18.7\\,[11.5, 25.7] & L & $<\\!10^{-6}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{2}{l}{\\textbf{Overall}} & \\textbf{41.0} & 41 & 41 & 30 & 43 & 50 & \\textbf{+34.0}\\,[31.3, 36.9] & --- & $<\\!10^{-105}$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\n\\vspace{2pt}\n{\\footnotesize $\\dagger$\\,Subject to information-addition confound; see \\S\\ref{sec:results} and Appendix~\\ref{app:confound}. $\\ddagger$\\,Lowest inter-judge agreement ($\\kappa = 0.385$, per-type calibration $n \\approx 17$); $\\Delta$token$\\,= 36.1$ words, subject to information-addition confound analogous to incompleteness; see \\S\\ref{sec:results}. Excluding incompleteness: pooled $\\Delta = +31.0$\\,pp (95\\% CI $[27.9, 34.1]$).}\n\\end{table*}\n\nThe six types are not interchangeable. An omnibus $\\chi^2$ test on ambiguous-condition violation rates rejects the uniformity null ($\\chi^2 = 28.02$, $df = 5$, $p = 3.6 \\times 10^{-5}$, Cram\\'{e}r's $V = 0.137$). Table~\\ref{tab:tost_sensitivity} reveals a margin-dependent equivalence structure: three types achieve equivalence at $\\pm 10$\\,pp, five at $\\pm 12$\\,pp, and all six at $\\pm 15$\\,pp; we present the full sensitivity analysis without anchoring to a single margin. The resulting partial ordering is exploratory and contingent on the $\\pm 15$\\,pp SESOI choice (Table~\\ref{tab:tost_sensitivity} sensitivity columns show instability below $\\pm 12$\\,pp). The NMI comparison ($0.23$ vs.\\ $0.01$) is threshold-independent. Table~\\ref{tab:main_results} and Figure~\\ref{fig:forest_plot} present the full breakdown.\n\nBootstrap clustering ($B = 10{,}000$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cluster}) confirms stable extremes across all margins. Cross-model concordance is moderate ($W = 0.398$, $p = 0.072$; Appendix~\\ref{app:kendall_w}). Per-type inter-judge agreement ranges from $\\kappa = 0.875$ (incompleteness) to $\\kappa = 0.385$ (authorization scope), with intermediate values for conditional precedence ($0.762$), lexical ($0.712$), scopal ($0.538$), and coreferential ($0.455$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_judge}). For coreferential and scopal, whose per-type CIs include zero ($n \\approx 16$), per-type effect estimates should be interpreted with caution; in matched-pair designs, random measurement error attenuates effects toward zero, making these estimates conservative. Authorization scope exhibits the widest cross-model range (12\\%--64\\%) and the lowest per-type judge agreement; conclusions about this type carry wider uncertainty than the pooled estimates.\n\nCross-model judge validation ($\\rho = 0.71$--$0.89$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_judge}) confirms type-ranking stability when model composition is held constant; the low two-group concordance ($\\rho = 0.09$; Appendix~\\ref{app:judge_group_ranking}) reflects differential vulnerability to authorization scope$^\\ddagger$ (12\\%--64\\% range), not ranking instability. The partial ordering has robust extremes---incompleteness as upper bound and coreferential as lower bound, replicated across seven models and three judges---with model-composition-dependent mid-tier structure.\n\nIncompleteness ranks highest in four of five models ($\\geq$46\\% in all seven; Appendix~\\ref{app:model_expansion}); its 49.2\\,pp effect is an upper bound (Appendix~\\ref{app:confound}). C3 (layer grouping) did not survive Holm--Bonferroni ($p = 0.017$, threshold $0.0125$).\n\n\\begin{figure}[!ht]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_forest_plot.pdf}\n\\caption{Per-type effect sizes ($\\Delta$pp) with Newcombe 95\\% CIs (overall: $+$34.0\\,pp, OR $= 15.1$). Types form a partial ordering: incompleteness as upper bound (conditional on information-addition confound), coreferential as lower bound, and a mid-range equivalence cluster. Mid-tier ordering is margin-dependent (\\S\\ref{sec:results}).}\n\\label{fig:forest_plot}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\label{sec:c4}C4 (model$\\times$type interaction) is exploratory and driven solely by authorization scope; details in Appendix~\\ref{app:c4_sans_auth}.\n\n",
        "numLines": 65,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 65
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/sections/experiments.tex"
  },

```json
{
  "parentUuid": "1ce1f07f-89a7-4e77-adb7-208bda598414",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-be-idea-gen-0519-4/projects/policy_ambiguity_safety/docs/paper/sections/taxonomy.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-be-idea-gen-0519-4/projects/policy_ambiguity_safety/docs/paper/sections/taxonomy.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Ambiguity Taxonomy}\n\\label{sec:taxonomy}\n\nWe adopt an inclusive definition of ambiguity that encompasses phenomena sometimes distinguished as underspecification or vagueness \\citep{poesio_artstein_2005,vandeemter_semantic_1996}: a policy clause is ambiguous when it admits multiple distinct interpretations available to a competent reader, including cases where the boundary between interpretations is gradient rather than discrete \\citep{wasow_ambiguity_2005,kennedy_vagueness_2007}. In the policy specification domain, these distinctions are less salient than the shared property of admitting multiple operationally distinct agent behaviors.\n\nTraditional NLP ambiguity classification focuses on within-sentence phenomena \\citep{wasow_ambiguity_2005}; our specification layer captures document-level policy gaps unstudied in the agent context. Our taxonomy provides an organizational framework of six types organized into two layers (Table~\\ref{tab:taxonomy}), selected from \\citet{li_ambiguity_taxonomy_2024}'s NLP taxonomy (11 types; full mapping in Appendix~\\ref{app:li_mapping}) based on three design criteria: (1)~theoretical attestation in prior RE literature \\citep{massey_ambiguity_re_2014,berry_kamsties_2004,drip_r_2026}, (2)~amenability to minimal-pair construction without altering clause semantics beyond the target ambiguity, and (3)~mutual exclusivity for unambiguous IV assignment. Types such as ellipsis and pragmatic implicature were excluded because they resist minimal-pair construction within the single-clause policy format.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\footnotesize\n\\caption{Six-type ambiguity taxonomy. Disambiguation channel indicates the primary mechanism required to resolve the ambiguity.}\n\\label{tab:taxonomy}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llll@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Type} & \\textbf{Layer} & \\textbf{Definition} & \\textbf{Disambig.\\ Channel} \\\\\n\\midrule\nLexical & Ling. & Word/phrase admits multiple senses & Context \\\\\nScopal & Ling. & Quantifier/operator scope underdetermined & Bracketing \\\\\nCoreferential & Ling. & Pronoun/NP referent is ambiguous & Resolution \\\\\nAuth.\\ Scope & Spec. & Permission boundary is vague & Boundary def. \\\\\nIncompleteness & Spec. & Required information is absent & Content addition \\\\\nCond.\\ Prec. & Spec. & Rules conflict under certain conditions & Priority rule \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}%\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Linguistic layer.} These types operate at the syntactic or semantic level and can, in principle, be resolved by contextual cues within the text.\n\\emph{Scopal} ambiguity involves quantifier, negation, or modal scope interactions (e.g., ``all items may not be returned'' admits universal-negation and negation-of-universal readings).\n\\emph{Lexical} ambiguity covers polysemy or homonymy (e.g., ``charge'' as financial cost vs.\\ penalty).\n\\emph{Coreferential} ambiguity involves unclear pronoun or NP reference (e.g., ``\\ldots which must be approved by a supervisor'' where ``which'' could refer to the verification or the refund).\n\n\\paragraph{Specification layer.} These types arise from pragmatic or discourse-level gaps requiring domain knowledge to resolve.\n\\emph{Authorization scope} covers vague permission or role boundaries (e.g., ``authorized personnel may issue refunds'' without specifying which personnel).\n\\emph{Incompleteness} denotes missing conditions or boundary cases (e.g., a return policy silent on damaged items); we control for its information-addition confound via logistic regression (\\S\\ref{sec:results}).\n\\emph{Conditional precedence} involves conflicting rules without priority ordering (e.g., ``premium customers receive free shipping'' vs.\\ ``orders under \\$50 require a shipping fee'').\n\nThe layer distinction reflects a design hypothesis motivated by prior typologies \\citep{massey_ambiguity_re_2014,berry_kamsties_2004}: linguistic ambiguity can in principle be partially resolved through tool responses or world knowledge, while specification ambiguity has no external resolution channel. This hypothesis motivates systematic coverage across both sources; its empirical status is reported in \\S\\ref{sec:results} (C3, inconclusive).\n\n\\paragraph{Epistemological status.}\nOur taxonomy uses \\emph{ambiguity} as a policy specification umbrella term encompassing two epistemologically distinct operations. We acknowledge that incompleteness occupies a contested boundary between ambiguity and underspecification in the RE literature; however, \\citet{berry_kamsties_2004}'s nocuous ambiguity taxonomy itself includes specification-level gaps, and \\citet{gleich_ambiguity_2010} treat missing information as ``implicit ambiguity.'' Five types involve \\emph{interpretation selection}: the text admits multiple readings and the agent commits to one. Incompleteness involves \\emph{information addition}: the text lacks necessary content entirely, and the agent confabulates it. This distinction manifests empirically: incompleteness is the only type unresponsive to prompt-based disambiguation ($-$3.4\\,pp, n.s.; \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}) because prompts cannot supply absent domain knowledge. We retain both under a single umbrella because agents process all six types identically---committing without flagging uncertainty---and the resulting violations produce comparable safety impact (\\S\\ref{sec:results}). This inclusive scope aligns with RE usage, where ambiguity and incompleteness frequently co-occur \\citep{dalpiaz_pinpointing_2018}.\n",
        "numLines": 42,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 42
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/sections/taxonomy.tex"
  },
  "type": "attachment",

```json
{
  "parentUuid": "6d84afb1-68a7-4edb-95c0-00c9dabff47f",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-be-idea-gen-0519-4/projects/policy_ambiguity_safety/docs/paper/sections/limitations.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-be-idea-gen-0519-4/projects/policy_ambiguity_safety/docs/paper/sections/limitations.tex",
        "content": "\\section*{Limitations}\n\\label{sec:limitations}\n\nOur ambiguity pairs are synthetically constructed, so the 34.0\\,pp effect is an upper bound; the SOPBench evaluation ($+$12.7\\,pp unweighted mean) provides a cross-benchmark lower bound. We evaluate seven API-accessible models across two customer-service domains at temperature $= 0$; a healthcare cross-domain pilot ($+$58.8\\,pp, $n = 250$; Appendix~\\ref{app:healthcare}) confirms the effect generalizes beyond customer service, though smaller models remain untested. Failure mode coding relies on LLM annotators ($\\kappa = 0.52$; human $\\kappa = 0.32$, rising to $0.41$ after collapsing the spurious-compliance/structural-misparse boundary; improved guidelines, prior round $0.15$), though the coder prompt excludes ambiguity type labels, preventing type--mechanism contamination. All experiments use temperature $= 0$, measuring each model's modal interpretation---the policy-relevant quantity agents will most likely commit to and the typical deployment setting for policy-governed agents. The matched-pair design partially mitigates temperature effects: any variance increase at higher temperatures applies to both conditions, so the treatment effect direction is unlikely to reverse. Moreover, the unambiguous-condition violation rate is already near floor (6.9\\%), so temperature-induced variance would predominantly affect the ambiguous condition, preserving or widening the gap. A temperature $= 0.7$ pilot across three model families (GPT-4.1, Claude Sonnet~4.6, DeepSeek-V3; Appendix~\\ref{app:temperature}) confirms that the treatment effect is robust (85--97\\% clause-level agreement with greedy decoding), though the remaining two models and extreme-temperature regimes remain untested; the per-model pilot ($n = 50$ pairs) is powered to detect effects $\\geq$24.6\\,pp at 80\\% power, so smaller attenuations at higher temperatures cannot be ruled out. Three additional scope limitations warrant disclosure: (1) three of the five judges share the OpenAI model family; however, GPT-5.4-judged and GPT-4.1-judged models show similar mean violation rates (38.0\\% vs.\\ 45.3\\%, driven by DeepSeek's high base rate rather than judge leniency; cross-model judge $\\kappa = 0.653$, McNemar $p = 0.606$), suggesting the 3:2 allocation does not systematically inflate convergence estimates; (2) authorization scope has the lowest per-type judge agreement ($\\kappa = 0.385$, $n \\approx 17$), so conclusions about this type carry wider uncertainty; (3) the coreferential type, whose variants differ only in referent specification, was excluded from both the mitigation and SOPBench replication analyses, limiting generalization for that type; and (4) the ICC analysis uses only within-family variation (GPT-4o/4o-mini), so the reported reliability bounds may not hold across architecturally diverse model pairs. All evaluated models are frontier-class ($\\geq$100B parameters or equivalent capability tier); generalization to smaller open-source models (7B--70B) remains untested. Future work should extend to non-customer-service domains and collect per-episode human mechanism annotations to validate the type--mechanism association.\n\n\\section*{Data and Code Availability}\nThe 300 clause pairs, evaluation pipeline, analysis scripts, and raw episode traces will be released upon publication at \\url{https://github.com/anonymous-nlp-2026/policy_ambiguity_safety}.\n",
        "numLines": 8,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 8
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/sections/limitations.tex"
  },
  "type": "attachment",

      "- claude: Catch-all for any task that doesn't fit a more specific agent. FleetView's default when no agent name is typed. (Tools: *)",
      "- Explore: Fast read-only search agent for locating code. Use it to find files by pattern (eg. \"src/components/**/*.tsx\"), grep for symbols or keywords (eg. \"API endpoints\"), or answer \"where is X defined / which files reference Y.\" Do NOT use it for code review, design-doc auditing, cross-file consistency checks, or open-ended analysis — it reads excerpts rather than whole files and will miss content past its read window. When calling, specify search breadth: \"quick\" for a single targeted lookup, \"medium\" for moderate exploration, or \"very thorough\" to search across multiple locations and naming conventions. (Tools: All tools except Agent, ExitPlanMode, Edit, Write, NotebookEdit)",
      "- general-purpose: General-purpose agent for researching complex questions, searching for code, and executing multi-step tasks. When you are searching for a keyword or file and are not confident that you will find the right match in the first few tries use this agent to perform the search for you. (Tools: *)",
      "- Plan: Software architect agent for designing implementation plans. Use this when you need to plan the implementation strategy for a task. Returns step-by-step plans, identifies critical files, and considers architectural trade-offs. (Tools: All tools except Agent, ExitPlanMode, Edit, Write, NotebookEdit)",
      "- statusline-setup: Use this agent to configure the user's Claude Code status line setting. (Tools: Read, Edit)"
    ],
